About the Author 
Ian Copsey is a veteran technical analyst having begun his career in markets 33 years 
ago in Barclays Bank trading rooms in London and Hong Kong - where he began his 
career as a technical analyst. 
He is author of Integrated Technical Analysis (1999) and Harmonic Elliott Wave 
(201 1) and Fractal Forecasting (201 5.) In 1 993 he moved to Tokyo to work with Dow 
Jones Telerate for 7 years. He is an experienced speaker at technical analysis 
workshops and seminars. 
Late Ian had experience in a start up company in Singapore before becoming an 
independent analyst, providing his daily Forex analysis, and later equity and metals 
reports. 
His work with Harmonic Elliott Wave has been applauded with some of the most 
accurate forecasts in the industry, in both Forex and equity markets. 
He forecast the May 201 5  high in the Dow Jones Industrial Index two years m 
advance that followed his forecast in July 201 0  when he suggested that the Dow 
Jones Index would complete its first 5-wave rally from inception.

INTRODUCTION I 1 
Introduction 
i'Ht, 
I 
llj ,,! 
I 
lj 
It has been fifteen years since my first book, Integrated Technical Analysis, was 
published in 1 999 and five years since I discovered the process of market 
development that I explained in my book Harmonic Elliott Wave. Effectively, 
this book is intended as an update to the process of forecasting financial markets 
using complementary techniques. In stating "financial markets" I have to qualify 
this by stating "fully liquid markets that are governed by mass psychology." By 
this I mean, not only traders, fund managers, retail traders but the whole spectrum 
of those who seek to make short or long term profit from the markets. I'd even 
include politicians who react emotionally to market movements and also central 
banks. I do not consider central banks as skewing the market but actually being 
part of the market emotion. 
The only exceptions I'd make is in the interest rate markets which are, to a 
large extent, controlled by central bank decisions, smaller stock markets where 
the risk of individuals being able to influence a market or individual stocks. This 
book is therefore about highly liquid markets where one party can only have 
limited influence. While many debate the influence of high frequency trading I 
have not found their activity having changed the basic structure of the Harmonic 
Elliott Wave ratio methodology. 
As I began writing Harmonic Elliott Wave, the fact that impulsive waves 
developed in 3-wave sequences rather than 5-wave as R.N. Elliott proposed in his 


=== PAGE 7 ===

2 I INTRODUCTION 
original theory, was very clear in my mind. I had also developed some insights as 
to the way projection ratios worked but not quite deeply enough. As I have used 
the technique over these past five years my understanding and methodology has 
been adapted to incorporate a deeper logic to the process. 
With these greater insights and experience of using Harmonic Elliott Wave I 
have absolutely no doubts that this methodology is the most powerful forecasting 
tool in the market. It is not without its problems. It is not an easy art to master. 
The markets are, after all, dynamic and multi-faceted that can be fickle and like to 
confuse. It's down to the experience I have gained over the past few years that 
has enabled a more integrated approach to forecasting. 
Of course, there are many, many detractors to the concept of forecasting. I 
come across them all the time. There are the steadfast, head in the sand traders 
who simply refuse to even consider the possibility of forecasting accurately. 
These tend to be traders rather than analysts, the psychological profile of which 
are very, very different. Their mindset is always geared to their belief that 
forecasting is an automated signal system. In particular, retail traders love the 
idea of forecasting and also treat it as a signal system. This is a very simplified 
and totally incorrect interpretation. 
The other detractors are those Elliotticians that steadfastly believe that it is 
sacrilege to even consider changing the structure of impulsive waves, irrespective 
of the fact that R.N. Elliott was effectively handicapped in being able to 
thoroughly research wave relationships given he had to make calculations long 
hand. I leave them to their devices but do note they are rarely able to forecast 
with accuracy. From the rest of R.N. Elliott's research I can only have the 
greatest admiration for his findings, in particular the corrective structures with 
which I can find no fault. He was, indeed, a brilliant man. 
The markets are complex and have an almost infinite number of complications 
that will defy any automated strategy. I have had some system designers ask me 
whether they can develop an automated indicator. These are always based on 
swing highs and swing lows and generating a "zigzag" indicator that identifies 
waves by these swing levels. However, they are totally illogical and generally fail 
miserably. As you read through this book you will find out why. 
Over the past few years using Harmonic Elliott Wave I have had a not 
insignificant number of direct hits in terms of forecasting in both long-term charts 
and short term. To me they are just the same, the process of identifying waves 
exactly identical. 
Perhaps the most obvious forecast was for the Dow Jones Industrial Index in 
Harmonic Elliott Wave. In July 201 0  when the DOW was around 9,500 I forecast 
a target around 1 2,800 that would be followed by a 20% price decline and then by 
a rally to the 2007 high at 14, 1 98. This developed perfectly. Indeed, before this 
point I would try and avoid forecasting equity markets because I had never been 
able to be right. It was this very first forecast I made in the DOW with Harmonic 


=== PAGE 8 ===

INTRODUCTION I 3 
Elliott Wave. At the end of this book I will detail where the DOW will end and 
complete the very first 5-wave rally from the inception of the DOW ... 
In this book, I am updating my findings on Harmonic Elliott Wave, explaining 
the logic (which is sadly lacking in the original structure) and providing greater 
insight of how to approach the task of tracking a forecast, the intricate 
methodology and how to fine tune the process using other complementary 
techniques that can be used as filters to understand whether the structural 
development is proceeding according to plan or breaking down. I shall also 
highlight the special features of Harmonic Elliott Wave - the amazing beauty of 
alternation, the frustrations of the wild card of the pack, the Wave B and how to 
cope with complications. 
Remember, forecasting is not simple. If it were, then everyone would be able 
to forecast. If everyone has the same position then the potential for larger playŬrs 
to hit stops would destroy the methodology. If you are not prepared to put in hard 
work into understanding how this process works, then the results will not come 
either. With dedication the outcome is far, far stronger. 
Ian Copsey

4 I THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE IMPULSIVE STRUCTURE 
Chapter One

THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE IMPULSIVE STRUCTURE 
Before being able to discuss the process of integrating technical analysis methods 
with a forecasting tool, it is important to describe the main forecasting 
methodology around which the integration of analysis is based. This chapter will 
cover the basic building blocks in the structure of an impulse wave within 
Harmonic Elliott Wave, as a foundation to take this forward into Chapter Three 
where I will discuss exactly how to define a wave, the characteristics and 
development. This is something that is loose and vague in the original Elliott 
Wave structure. Therefore, the reader may want to review this chapter once again 
after Chapter Three. 
I am also insistent that Harmonic Elliott Wave is a price indicator and not a 
system that generates automated signals. As I go through the way in which it 
works I think this will be come self-evident. It is vitally important that if you 
have any thoughts that this will generate unlimited profits independently, that you 
dispel this immediately. I wrote on my blog one day that I find the fractal process 
of Harmonic Elliott Wave rather similar to a Sudoku problem. We need to work 
across time frames, be aware of the multitude of alternatives that can develop and 
yet all end up at the same end result. If you have not worked with Harmonic 


=== PAGE 10 ===

THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE IMPULSIVE STRUCTURE I 5 
Elliott Wave yet, then this may sound a bit puzzling, but hopefully this should be 
made much clearer as this book progresses. 
Harmonic Elliott Wave is a basic framework that has a wide range of potential 
solutions, like an individual Sudoku square of nine, but which must slot in with 
the other related Sudoku squares of nine. As we go through each step I shall 
describe the methodology of how to cope with this process. 
I shall not be describing traditional Elliott Wave. I have happily forgotten the 
confusing process, one that is lacking in logic and something I cannot even begin 
to try and attempt anymore. Therefore, these sections on Harmonic Elliott Wave 
will be the sole source of the forecasting methodology. I do use price patterns -
reversal and continuation - but only as secondary tools to confirm the 
expectations of Harmonic Elliott Wave. I shall dedicate a section to corrective 
patterns because they are vital, and to point out why some rather esoteric 
corrective structures many use in Elliott Wave are totally incorrect and more a 
function of the 3-wave add-ons which Harmonic Elliott Wave encompasses 
within the standard basic structure. 
As a basic introduction, I shall merely state that I have made no changes to the 
corrective structures that R.N. Elliott proposed apart from the construction of 
Wave A and Wave C. Both are comprised of 5 waves but within definition of the 
impulsive development where Wave (i), (iii) and (v) are all constructed of 3-
waves, in (a)(b)(c). It is the Wave A and Wave C that constructs all other waves, 
both impulsive and corrective. This may appear confusing but once the 
understanding of how impulsive waves are defined, it will be obvious how they 
are distinct from corrective waves. 
THE SIMPLE IMPULSIVE STRUCTURE 
Fig 1-1 The Harmonic Elliott Wave impulse wave 
(A) or (C) 
(v) 
(c) 


=== PAGE 11 ===

6 I THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE IMPULSIVE STRUCTURE 
I use the nomenclature of (i), (ii) etc. merely to ensure that this is not confused 
with R.N. Elliott's structure. As you can see, each of Waves (i), (iii) and (v) is 
constructed in 3-waves - (a)(b)(c). In this manner, the structure of every 
impulsive wave is exactly the same although with different ratio length, types of 
corrective waves and with the variety of ways the lower degree waves can 
develop. 
Note: 
The only waves that can be constructed of 5-waves are Wave (a) and Wave (c). 
Along with the corrective waves, these are the core components of all other 
structures. 
Additionally, the benefit of Harmonic Elliott Wave is that it eliminates 
extended waves, failed fifths and leading/ending triangles. These features of the 
original structure were merely aberrations that were required to accommodate the 
additional waves that R.N. Elliott proposed. As mentioned earlier, the Harmonic 
Elliott Wave structure also absorbs the amazing range of running corrections, 
massive expanded flats and curious hybrid irregular corrections that are merely 
there to justify a count. Again, when I discuss the wave relationships this will 
become more apparent. 
An example of a simple 5-wave rally is shown in Fig 1 -2 and displays the rally 
I forecast in my book, Harmonic Elliott Wave, for the DOW Jones Industrial 
Index to reach the 12,800 area before it dropped by 20%. 
Fig 1 -2. A simple 5-wave rally in the Dow Jones Industrial Index

Fig 1-3 shows, in the same manner as R.N. Elliott's structure, that the simple 
structures also provide the building blocks for the complex structure within the 
harmonic structure. As we move forward you will see how this structure is truly 
fractal with lower degree targets required to approach higher degree targets. Also 
note that compared to traditional Elliott Wave, there are no extended waves, no 
failed fifths and no leading or ending triangles. This provides a single consistent 
5-wave structure. 
While I will discuss the topic of ratios used in Harmonic Elliott Wave later, 
these waves are controlled by a series of ratio clusters that define a 5-wave 
development. 
Fig 1-4 below shows how the example in Fig 1 -2 that displayed the 5-wave 
rally from the Wave (b) to the Wave (c) I (iii) fit within the larger rally from the 
2009 low. This also shows the Wave (iv) correction that I forecast in July 20 1 0  of 
20%, the actual decline being 1 9% before rallying back to the 2007 high. Each 
Wave (a), Wave (c), Wave -a- and Wave -c- will need to be comprised of 5-
waves.

This 5-wave rally formed the Wave (( A )) of Wave (( V )) within the major

extended waves, no diagonal triangles and no failed fifths. While wave 
relationships will be discussed later, each of the wave degrees (fractals) will need 
to adhere to the ratio clusters. In Fig 1 -4, where it is possible to identify lower 
degree waves, I have placed a label. 
R.N. ELLIOTT'S RULES 
Just as R.N. Elliott provided just three simple rules, the same are used in 
Harmonic Elliott Wave: 
• 
Wave 2 will not move below/above the start of Wave 1 
./ 
• 
Wave 3 will never be the shortest of Waves 1 ,  3 & 5 
./ 
• 
The extremes of Wave 1 and Wave 4 will never overlap 
./ 
However, in Harmonic Elliott Wave there is one additional rule: 
• 
The Wave (iv) will not break the Wave (b) of Wave (iii) and normally 
not get close N.B. (Ref: Alternation) 
Just touching on this additional Harmonic Elliott Wave rule, take a look back 
at Fig 1-4 and where it stalled in relation to the Wave (b) I (iii). Later, when I

where a Wave (iii) must reach in order that a deeper Wave (iv) does not approach 
the Wave (b) I (iii).

Guideline 1 
The relationships between Wave [ii] and Wave [iv], also Wave i and Wave ii, 
will be discussed later but as can be seen in Fig 1 -5, the Wave [ii] and Wave ii in 
USDJPY were both deep and long, evidencing a complex correction. As the 
follow-through higher in Wave iii and Wave [iii] developed, it can be seen that 
the Wave iv and Wave [iv] were both very brief and shallow. This has a strong

tendency across all wave degrees, from the lower degrees to the multi-year wave 
degrees. 
Ill 
Fig 1-6. A simple, shallow Wave (ii) compared to a deep, complex Wave (iv) 
Equally, if a Wave ii is very brief and simple and shallow, then the Wave iv will 
be deep and complex. This is seen in the Dow Jones Industrial Index in Fig 1 -6. 
In contrast to the comparison of the deep Wave ii (and Wave [ii]) and shallow 
Wave iv (and Wave [iv]) in Fig 5-1 ,  the opposite is true when the Wave ii is 
simple and shallow while the Wave iv is deep and complex. This tends to see 
quite a contrast in terms of the time taken to complete the corrections. 
Guideline 2 
The second guideline R.N. Elliott provided was the case when, following a Wave 
C in a corrective ABC structure, price will retest the prior Wave B. Since 
Harmonic Elliott Wave is based on 3-wave structures (a)(b)(c) in all waves, this 
guideline is only appropriate within a corrective structure. (However, there is just 
one instance where price returns to a Wave (b) but I shall cover that below when 
considering Elliott's third guideline.)

THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE I MPULSIVE STRUCTURE I ll 
Fig 1 -7. Return to the prior Wave -b- following a triple three decline in the DOW 
Following a triple three decline in Wave -iv- in the Dow Jones Industrial Index 
in Fig 1-7, price recovered back to retest the span of the third Wave -b-. This is, 
perhaps, an unusual example because the final Wave -c- was the most aggressive, 
ending in a spike low. Due to this it would be unlikely to see a perfectly direct 
recovery but the Wave [iii] of Wave -a- did generate a correction, even if shallow. 
In these circumstances there are likely to be earlier corrections as can be seen. 
The first was to around the high of the prior Wave iv and the second to the span 
of Wave b of Wave iii. The Wave b of Wave iii doesn't normally provide any 
support or resistance, but in this case it was the previous swing high. Therefore, 
we should expect a reaction, even if minor. 
Guideline 3 
Coming to the third of Elliott's guidelines: 
After completion of an extended Wave (5) the first retracement will return to 
the extreme of the Wave 2 of Wave (5) 
- since there is no Wave 2 in Wave (v), it is impossible for the first retracement 
to retest the extreme of the Wave 2 of Wave ( 5). This guideline is replaced by the 
first reversal retracing into the span of Wave (b) of Wave (v).

to develop quite quickly. 
ALTERNATION 
I have covered this topic above when discussing R.N. Elliott's second 
guideline: If Wave 2 is complex then Wave 4 will be simple (and vice versa). 
However, while detailed above, the guideline doesn't really require depth to be 
taken into consideration. When you look at Elliotticians' charts, depth is never 
really considered - even when prominent Elliotticians provide their counts. It is 
just simply ignored in favor of using convenient swing highs and swing lows. 
However, in Harmonic Elliott Wave alternation develops in the vast majority 
of cases in both complexity and depth. Where there is any shortfall in depth, this 
is normally compensated by either the Wave (b) of Wave (iii) or the Wave (b) of 
Wave (v). 


=== PAGE 18 ===

THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE I MPULSIVE STRUCTURE 1 13 
While there will be many more examples over the course of this book, let me 
provide a couple of examples that show how these develop. The first is a repeat of 
Fig 1-6 that showed the complex impulsive structure in the Dow Jones Industrial 
Index. This is shown once again below: 
Ill 
Fig 1-9 The Dow Jones Industrial Index displaying alternation in depth and 
complexity 
While I will cover the wave relationships later, the Wave (ii) in this rally was 
very shallow. It was the basis of the Wave (i) and Wave (ii) that allowed me to 
estimate the Wave (iii) target. In addition, because of the inherent relationships of 
the ratios in the Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) it was also possible to determine the area 
where the Wave (iv) will stall. This worked perfectly in terms of depth and while 
the Wave (iv) was not a complex correction (see Chapter 2) it was comprised of a 
double zigzag within which the Wave x was an irregular triangle. 
As I will describe later, when eyeballing a new chart, one of the obvious points 
to watch for is the contrasting depths of Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) that provides a 
very strong clue as to the structure. Another example is in the decline from the 
Wave 1\E high in EURUSD following the initial decline from the 1 .3993 in Fig 
1 .8. This saw a very shallow Wave ii that then required a deeper Wave iv. 
I will discuss more about natural support and resistance in a later chapter.

Following a sharp reversal higher in the Dow Jones Industrial Index that saw a 
shallow Wave ii (towards the bottom left of the chart), price rallied in quite a 
complicated manner to the Wave iii from where a sharp, but deep Wave iv was 
seen. The subsequent rally in Wave v formed the daily Wave -iii- high within the 
Wave (iii). 
Thus, once a Wave ii has been formed, it is possible to estimate the Wave iii 
targets through the Harmonic Elliott Wave spreadsheet and also anticipate the 
area of the Wave iv. 
THE ROLE OF THE WAVE B WITHIN IMPULSIVE WAVES 
Until now I have attempted to show charts that look much like the image in Fig 
1-1 that shows a clear development. However, as you can imagine, there are 
plenty of examples that are a lot more complicated. The market is dynamic and 
while the impulsive waves (iii), (iv) and (v) can generally be identified, there will 
always be a variety of different influences that can trigger complications. The 
Wave (b) is a key wave that copes with the volatility, absorbing minor or major 
price shocks, surprising political statements or unexpected economic data. It can 
be a simple, shallow correction, a deep direct correction or can take on any type 
of complex correction, which will be described in Chapter 2. 


=== PAGE 20 ===

THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE I MPULSIVE STRUCTURE l 1s 
Occasionally, they can be relatively straightforward but conversely they can 
also test our patience. A variety of examples will be seen in all charts in this 
book. It would be helpful for the reader to observe these and note how they work.

Fig 1-1 1 A more complicated 5-wave rally in EURUSD 
I spent a couple of weeks demonstrating how an impulsive Harmonic Elliott 
Wave rally was developing in EURUSD on an Elliott Wave forum. All were 
bearish. Elliotticians on Linkedin were bearish quoting how the rally was too 
choppy to be an impulsive move. However, I correctly forecast this, even 
providing the Wave [iii] and Wave [iv] targets two weeks in advance. It just so 
happened that the Wave [iii] and Wave [iv] occurred over a U.S. Non Farm 
Payrolls release, which demonstrates the stability of the structural development 
despite market moving economic releases. 
One of the elements in this chart is the depth of the Wave [b] of Wave [iii]. 
Many Elliotticians think I am crazy using this methodology but, as I will 
demonstrate later in the section where I explain the ratio methodology, I will 
explain how these work and the common frustrations of Wave b' s in general. 
Wave b' s are the "wild card" of the pack. They have no standard ratios and 
basically act as a buffer that allows the market to absorb information, for market 
participants to dally while they make up their mind what is happening. In most 
positions they can be as shallow as 9% or 14.6o/o or as deep as 1 00%, depending 
on the position in the wave structure. 
As an example of a very shallow Wave b correction, my favorite is in the S&P 
Index. This is an example of how the R.N. Elliott structure fails. In all charts of 
this rally I have seen the major swing high in the middle of the chart labeled

extremely shallow Wave -b- was correct because, two days before the higher 
Wave iii (marked below the Wave -iii-,) I forecast the Wave iii high, the area of 
the Wave iv, the Wave -iii- target and Wave -iv- retracement area. This forecast 
lasted over the following two months. I was not too far wrong with the Wave -v- I 
(iii) high also. 
This highlights the fractal nature of Harmonic Elliott Wave and how merely 
using major swing highs and swing lows are not necessarily reflective of the 
maJor waves. 
It is also possible to note that within each Wave A and Wave C there is a 
distinct 5-wave count, of course all adhering to the ratio structure (discussed 
later) and with lower degree targets matching higher degree targets. 
As a second example of this, I shall repeat the rally in EURUSD and add the 
lower degree wave count where possible. I hope this chart in Fig 1 . 13 below can 
be seen clearly. Unfortunately, I have had to put in a great deal of information 
within a tight space so it may be difficult to see all the points. I have attempted to

cases, such as the Wave [i] there is not enough detail in the initial sharp rally. The 
same applies to the final Wave [ c] I [ v] that really has little information with the 
2-hour bars absorbing much of the information. However, the basic concept is 
there. The lower degrees build the higher degrees.

While I have focused on the Wave b of Wave iii, the same feature of an 
unknown depth in the correction in a Wave b, is true in both the Wave i and 
Wave v. However, while in a Wave i or Wave v the retracement in Wave b can be 
up to 100% the Wave b of Wave iii has a general retracement maximum of 
76.4%-85.4% - although I have occasionally seen 90%. 
This chapter has hopefully provided the basic foundation of how the impulsive 
section of the Harmonic Elliott Wave structure develops, the simple and complex 
structures including the vagueness of the Wave b. There is more to discuss but 
before I move onto how to identify waves we should discuss corrective waves. 


=== PAGE 23 ===

18 I CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES 
Chapter Two 
i'Ht, 
I 
Ill ,,1 
I 
jl 
CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES 
Corrections. You either dislike them or you hate them. I have been asked on quite 
a few occasions whether Harmonic Elliott Wave makes corrections any easier. 
The answer is "sort of' but corrections are there for a number of reasons. This can 
be because the market has temporarily lost its way, has accumulated a one sided 
trade for too long, is confused by conflicting economic releases or waiting for 
them, and even because of seasonal low liquidity. There are probably more 
reasons but the basic fact is that there will always be periods when the market 
stalls and begins to move sideways or to retrace a prior trending move. 
In terms of the complexity they vary enormously. Some are just brief 
corrections, sometimes a little more complex and when these develop in the lower 
timeframes they can be absorbed with relative ease as long as you know which 
move is being corrected. The more serious problems arise when moving up the 
time frames. 


=== PAGE 24 ===

CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES I 19 
Think of this in terms of the Sudoku squares I used to describe the way the 
impulsive structures slot together through the lower and higher wave degrees. 
While impulsive moves can be complicated, when compared to daily or weekly 
corrections, impulsive waves are relatively simple. We can probably use the 
example of nine blocks of nine squares. However, within the higher timeframe 
corrections a suitable comparison would be taking nine blocks of nine squares 
and expand them to 36 blocks of nine squares - or even more . . .  The potential for 
greater losses rises in the larger timeframe due to the wider range that can cause 
misjudgments that generate larger losses. Within the lower time frame we can 
normally judge the limits of the correction. However, in each, the corrective 
structures are equally complex. 
Of course, like impulsive waves, corrective waves are also built from the base 
3-wave structure - an (a)(b)(c). The Wave (a) and Wave (c) are the only waves 
that are comprised of 5-waves. Even in these 5-wave sequences the same ratios 
apply. That's one positive point. It is easier, in general, to identify a 5-wave 
sequence. That's all well and good, but in general the corrective sequences within 
the corrective waves tend to become more elastic, stretch the corrective ratios and 
also the Wave ii and Wave iv relationships to more towards the limits compared 
to impulsive waves within an trending sequence. 
NOTE: 
Corrective waves also like to play little tricks. For example, a triple three is a 
sequence of three separate (a)(b)(c) moves divided by a Wave (x). I not so 
infrequently see what looks to be a relatively minor 3 -wave follow through in a 
final (a)(b)(c) only for that minor 3-wave move to be the first leg of an expanded 
flat, the recycling back to form the Wave c of the expanded flat and then followed 
by a larger (a )(b)( c) to complete the triple three. Therefore, they do need a 
greater level of concentration and attention that can be identified by using other 
technical analysis methods to filter the development. 
If this all seems much too much, then do not despair. The more you suffer from 
these, the more street smart you will become. Also, in some corrections there can 
be fairly obvious limits to extent of the move. The easiest, by far, is the Wave (iv) 
that, through alternation, generally has an identifiable range. Some Wave (b)'s of 
Wave (iii)'s can be judged relatively well if the Wave (ii) was shallow, the Wave 
(a) of (iii) not too long and therefore the Wave (c) needs to reach a minimum 
Wave (iii) target that normally (but unfortunately not always) implies a shallower 
Wave (b). 
For now, that's probably enough of an introduction and we should now begin 
to go through the different types of corrective waves. These are comprised of: 


=== PAGE 25 ===

20 I CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES 
"Simple corrections" 
A simple zigzag 
A double zigzag 
A triple three 
- an (a)(b)(c) 
- an (a)(b)(c)- (x) -(a)(b)(c) 
- an (a )(b)( c)- ( x) -(a )(b)( c)- ( x) -(a )(b)( c) 
"Complex corrections" 
A flat correction 
- an initial (a)(b)(c) followed by a move back to the start of 
the move and then retracing to end of the first (a )(b)( c) 
An expanded flat - an initial (a)(b)(c) followed by a move back to beyond the 
original directional move and then retracing to end of the first 
(a)(b )(c) 
A triangle 
- an initial (a )(b)( c) followed by 4 further legs that converge 
into an apex before breaking in the direction of the underlying 
direction 
NOTE: 
In the charts used in this book I use a labeling system for complex corrections: 
Triangles: 
Irregular Triangles 
Flat Corrections 
Expanded Flats 
1\A, 1\B, 1\C, 1\D, 1\E (lower case or upper case) 
EFAA, EFAB, EFAC, EF/\D, EFAE 
FA, FB, FC 
EFA, EFB, EFC 
I prefer to use this labeling convention instead of the normal Wave (a) for the 
first leg of a complex because it is easier to quickly understand what structure is 
being used. 
NB. A triangle can also have one leg as irregular. This would see one leg 
behave similar to an expanded flat, apparently "poking out" and then returning 
into the triangle. 
I would voice one warning that the running triangles used by traditional 
Elliotticians are completely incorrect and a product of "making a count fit." This 
is because their wave count can't be justified in any other way. In fact, it merely 
confirms the 3-wave impulsive structure in Harmonic Elliott Wave. After 
describing each corrective structure I will provide examples of counts I have seen 
with running triangles and how these are naturally incorporated into Harmonic 
Elliott Wave as part of the impulsive structure. 


=== PAGE 26 ===

CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES I 21 
Correction Rules 
• Corrections will develop in the following waves: Wave (ii), Wave (iv), 
Wave (b) and Wave (x). 
• 
All types of corrections can take the form of any corrective structure with 
the exception of Wave (ii) that will never develop as a triangle. 
• Any correction may include a lower degree corrective structure within each 
larger degree corrective wave. 
SIMPLE CORRECTIONS 
A single Zigzag 
Fig 2-1 A single Zigzag 
(a) 
(c) 
v 
c 
Very simply a Zigzag is a single (a)(b)(c) development. The Wave (a) and 
Wave (c) will always be comprised of 5-waves, constructed in a consistent 
structure, defined by ratios and with the corrective waves (Wave ii, Wave b and 
Wave iv) all developing in one of the corrective forms, and indeed, maybe a 
complex correction that will require more detailed analysis. Fig 2- 1 displays a 5-
wave Wave (a) and Wave (c) divided by a simple zigzag in Wave (b).

This 3-wave recovery was a simple ABC, each of Wave A and Wave C 
developing in 5-waves. Note that the Wave B was comprised of an expanded flat 
in Wave EF A, Wave EFB and then back lower in Wave EFC. 
A Double Zigzag 
Fig 2-3 A Double Zigzag 
(c) 
v 
c 
As described, a Double Zigzag is basically two Zigzags divided by an 
intervening Wave (x). Note that the Wave (b)'s and Wave (x) can develop in any 
corrective pattern including complex corrections. Normally, the Wave (x) does 


=== PAGE 28 ===

CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES I 23 
not breach the Wave (b) but there are some occasions where this is not the case. It 
is therefore prudent to allow for deeper retracements in Wave (x). 
Fig 2-4 The decline in the Dow Jones Industrial Index in the Wave (iv) 
This is the Wave (iv) decline in the Dow Jones Industrial Index that I forecast 
in my book, Harmonic Elliott Wave, in terms of depth. It was not an easy 
correction to follow. The first decline clearly developed in 5-waves to form a 
Wave A and followed by a deep Wave B. The Wave C actually approached the 
Wave (iv) retracement area quite closely. Initially, because the Wave (ii) had 
been relatively brief, I began to feel that we were seeing a flat correction 
(considering the 3-wave decline) that would retest the Wave (iii) high and then 
reverse back to the targeted Wave (iv). 
As you can see, this did not happen. Instead we saw a 3-wave recovery, 
followed by a correction and I had anticipated a double zigzag or triple three back 
to Wave (iii). Indeed, I was encouraged by the subsequent rally that broke above 
the first 3-wave recover. However, the market collapsed again and having seen 
this I suspected a possible triangle with the Wave AC as irregular. It was not a 
pretty structure for a triangle but, in the end, generated a final ABC to the Wave 
(iv) target area. From there we have seen the market rally to new highs. 
So, that completed a double zigzag but clearly with an extremely complicated 
internal development. Clearly, the process of working through corrective patterns 
is very intense and requires a good deal of observation and awareness of the 
implications of breaks, and the alternative outcomes. 


=== PAGE 29 ===

24 I CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES 
A Triple Three 
(C) 
v 
c 
Fig 2-5. A Triple Three 
It will come as no surprise that a Triple Three is merely a Double Zigzag with 
an additional Wave (x) and a third Zigzag. These can be quite difficult to follow 
because corrective wave development tends to contain a lot more lower degree 
noise along with the risk of complex corrections within the Wave B and Wave X 
positions. 
Fig 2.6. A Triple Three decline in USDJPY 


=== PAGE 30 ===

CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES I 25 
Early in 201 2, on the basis of the major cycles, I had already forecast a rally in 
USDJPY towards the 1 00- 1 02 area. It eventually completed the rally at 1 03.73. I 
had been expecting a much deeper correction but what developed is shown in Fig 
2-6. It is not possible to display the lower degree structure in many instances 
because much of the information is concealed within the hourly chart but can 
only be recognized in the 5-minute chart. However, as can be seen, the completed 
correction came in a Triple Three and, to date, we have never seen a move below 
this 93.78 low. In fact, by the time this book is published USDJPY has reached 
above 125.00. 
When beginning a correction we never really know for sure what type of 
corrective structure will eventually develop. We may have an idea of the area 
where the correction may stall, but in advance we basically need to observe the 
first movement and must judge how this develops. Particularly with deep 
corrections, due to the 3-wave methodology, we also have to be able to 
differentiate between a larger ABC move - in which the first move will always be 
3-waves - and the potential for a 3-wave move to construct a Wave -i- and later 
Wave -ii- from where it will develop into a 5-wave move in a Wave A. Initially 
this could provide some uncertainty but must be judged in terms of the depth of 
the expected correction. Following the Wave A, a deeper Wave B is then required 
followed by a Wave C. and then towards the target retracement area. However, 
while I have not yet described the ratio methodology, we need to ensure that the 
Wave -c- targets match a Wave -iii- target. If this does not happen, or alternation 
fails between what we may have considered the Wave -ii- and Wave -iv-, we 
would have to work with a triple three structure which does not require deeper 
corrections. 
While the Zigzags, Double Zigzags and Triple Threes are considered to be 
simple corrections, in reality they can be anything but simple when working 
through the development. . .  It is important to remember that while the Wave A 
and Wave C of a zigzag must adhere to the ratio structure, the corrective 
structures that develop in Wave B and Wave X can be of any simple, or complex 
correction. An example of such a complicated correction is the Wave (b) of Wave 
(v) in the Dow Jones Industrial Index on its way to complete a 5-wave rally to the 
final high from inception. This Wave (b) developed in an irregular triangle over a 
period of 4 months - and which followed the Wave (a) of Wave (v) that took only 
1 month. 
A second example of a triple three occurred in Gold back in the early months 
of 2012. Following a decline to the low labeled "fa," it looked as if there was a 
sharp rally that, in general, would see many Elliotticians jumping on the 
bandwagon considering it to be an impulsive rally that is seen in Fig 2-7. 


=== PAGE 31 ===

26 I CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES 
Fig 2-7. A triple three recovery in Gold within the Wave fb 
KEY TIP 
I am commonly asked, "If impulsive waves in Harmonic Elliott Wave are 
constructed of 3-waves within Waves (i), (iii) and (v) then what's the difference 
between impulsive waves and triple three's?" The answer is very clear - it is 
exceptionally rare for triple three's to adhere to the impulsive projection 
requirements and also alternation. 
It was quite a difficult rally to follow, as often is the case in corrections, but as 
can be seen in Fig 2-7 it was very direct. Referring to the description of 
alternation in Chapter 1 ,  it is clear that there was no alternation between a 
potential Wave ii and Wave iv. This is where Harmonic Elliott Wave really helps 
the understanding of whether the move is corrective or impulsive. 
To put this retracement into perspective, I have added Fig 2-8 (below) that 
displays the rally in more details in the 2-hour chart. There are two main clues 
that clearly demonstrate that this was a corrective move. Firstly, as will be 
described in Chapter 3, the balance of the three (a)(b)(c) waves do not conform to 
the projection ratio requirements. Secondly, as mentioned above, there is no 
alternation present within the combined (a)(b)(c) waves. However, observing the 
breakdown in the Waves (a) and (c), there is alternation in the individual waves.

COMPLEX CORRECTIONS 
Complex corrections are a group of three-wave structures that combine to form 
the three types of complex corrective structures listed below. Indeed, I tend to 
find them more straightforward because they will always begin with an (a)(b)(c) 
structure. When looking around at Elliotticians' charts I find this ignored 
completely because it suits the structure that they want to use. 
I will cover this later but will just pass on a personal experience that has 
remained with me since December 1 992 when I visited Elliott Wave 
International's office in Gainesville, Georgia. I was considering the possibility of 
joining them and took a basic test. I was still very much a rookie Elliottician at 
the time and was corrected by Dave Allman when I labeled a complex correction 
that did not have a 3-wave Wave A. However, to this day I frequently see 
triangles being labeled that do not begin with a 3-wave Wave A. I will provide 
examples later and explain how Harmonic Elliott Wave caters for these situations. 
Complex corrections have three variations: 
• A flat correction 
• An expanded flat correction 
• A triangle 
Each of them will first develop a 3-wave correction that will be relatively sharp 
and towards a valid retracement area. Should the 3-wave move take a longer 


=== PAGE 33 ===

28 I CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES 
period of time, extending sideways in a longer move, such as in Fig 2-2 then it 
will more likely be just a simple Zigzag. The following descriptions follow the 
development of a sharp 3-wave correction. 
A Flat Correction 
A flat correction is basically as it sounds. Following the end of a directional wave 
(either in an impulsive sequence or within a multi-Zigzag correction) a reversal is 
seen that is formed by an (a)(b)(c). I use the labeling of Wave FA (ie. Flat Wave 
A.) Once this Wave FA is complete, price will then move back to the end of the 
directional wave and stall. This will form Wave FB from where price will reverse 
back to the Wave FA (and normally just beyond) in a 5-wave move. 
Fig 2-9. A flat correction 
Correction 
completion 
In Fig 2-9, the Wave FB has been shown as developing in a triple three with a 
flat correction in the second Wave b position. The reversal lower in Wave FC 
then develops in a 5-wave sequence back to (normally) just extending through the 
Wave FA although there are occasions where it fails just before reaching it. 
Normally, if we can judge a possible valid retracement ratio it can be used as an 
approximate target. 
While I cover this later, do be aware that within a Wave FC (and also Wave 
EFC) the corrections can be a lot more volatile and the chances of a deep Wave b 
of Wave iii is very high. 


=== PAGE 34 ===

Fig 2-10 shows a flat correction in a Wave -ii- with the Wave FC falling 
slightly below the Wave FA although the Wave FB is almost exactly at the level 
of the Wave FA. The Wave FC is rather complicated, as often happens, with the 
Wave c of Wave iii developing in a particularly choppy manner. I have attempted 
to display the count as best I can in a lighter grey. The black Wave iii and Wave 
iv cannot be seen as they are caught within the hourly bar. 
In practice, I find flat corrections less common than expanded flat corrections. 
An Expanded Flat Correction 
A flat correction is basically very similar to a flat correction except the middle 
corrective leg (that stalled at the directional high/low in a flat correction) exceeds 
the extreme and stalled just beyond before reversing. 
Following the end of a directional wave (either in an impulsive sequence or 
within a multi-Zigzag correction) a reversal is seen that is formed by an (a)(b)(c). 
I use the labeling of Wave EF A (ie. Expanded Flat Wave A.) Once this Wave 
EF A is complete, price will then move back to beyond the end of the directional 
wave. This will form Wave EFB from where price will reverse back to the Wave 
EFA (and normally just beyond) in a 5-wave move.

As mentioned above, I find these more common than flat corrections. Equally, 
the Wave EFB can be extremely complicated with erratic and choppy 
development that can include any form of corrective patterns, from zigzags to 
complex corrective patterns within Wave b, Wave x and Wave iv. 
Fig 2-1 2. An expanded flat Wave (b) in the NASDAQ Composite Index 


=== PAGE 36 ===

CORRECTIVE STRUCTURES I 31 
Fig 2- 12 displays an expanded flat Wave (b) in the NASDAQ Composite Index. 
Note how the Wave EFA develops in 3-waves and the Wave EFB in a double 
zigzag within which the first Wave [b] develops as an irregular triangle. 
Triangles 
Like their complex correction counterparts, triangles also begin with an 
(a)(b)(c) wave that forms Wave /\A. From this point we should see three further 
waves - Wave 1\B, Wave 1\C and Wave 1\D that normally develop sideways in a 
converging range across which we normally can draw the triangle lines. These 
three waves can be comprised of any combination of corrective structures, rather 
similar to those seen in a Wave FB of a flat correction or Wave EFB in an 
expanded flat. The final leg, the Wave 1\E must always develop in a 3-wave move 
in (a)(b)(c). The Wave 1\E should not be too close to the apex of the triangle 
otherwise it becomes unstable with the resultant risk of either a flat or expanded 
flat correction suddenly materializing. 
These are particularly flighty patterns. When they work, they work well. 
However, being a corrective pattern, I find they can often morph into other, more 
complex, patterns. It is also not uncommon that it looks like a (say) Wave 1\C has 
completed only to see a minor correction and followed by a mini-break, even 
sometimes by just 1 -5 points, to then reverse back into the next leg. They should 
be treated with great respect and care. 
Fig 2-13. A converging triangle

completion

that Wave /\A will always be in 3-waves, Waves /\B, Wave /\C and Wave /\D can 
be composed of any combination of corrective waves while Wave /\E must be in 
3-waves.

Fig 2-1 4  displays a long converging bearish triangle in gold. The break of the

However, not all triangles are as tidy as this. They can see wider swings 
between legs that are difficult to confine to between converging lines. Others, 
such as the example in Fig 2.4 of the Wave (iv) decline in the Dow Jones 
Industrial Index develop with one leg irregular, similar to an expanded flat. 
However, all are valid as long as the Wave /\A and Wave /\E is in three legs and 
the intervening legs are all comprised of any permitted collection of corrective 
structures. Of course, ascending and descending triangles are two more versions 
of triangle structures. 
As a special note on triangles, they really do require a great deal of care. There 
can be a great deal of "noise" in a triangle that can mislead by a final 3 -wave 
move in a triple three - but then which turns out to be an expanded flat that is 
then followed by yet another 3-wave move. It is also not uncommon for legs of 
triangles to whip past the prior high (or low) and then reverse, causing a loss to be 
made. Thus, great care and attention is required.

Corrective Wave Summary 
To summarize corrective structures, they are difficult but not all the time. 
Indeed, Wave (iv)'s are normally pretty straightforward to forecast. There can 
still be twists and turns but the target retracement areas are mostly identifiable. 
The harder corrections to judge are the Wave (ii)'s and Wave (b)'s that both have 
a wide range of retracement levels to work with. Wave (x) is generally a little 
easier than Wave (ii) and Wave (b) since, by and large, they tend not to retrace 
beyond the Wave (b) but there can be a few exceptions. 
However, as we go forward through the next chapter that describes the way 
ratios can be used to identify particular waves, it will be possible to begin to grasp 
how the process of wave identification has a much lower level of subjectivity and 
greater objectivity.

DEFINING WAVES 
There is long standing joke about Elliott Wave. Actually, perhaps it's not a joke 
but a reality. It reads: "Put five Elliotticians into a room with one chart and they 
will come out with twenty wave counts." Perhaps that is a bit excessive, but on 
second thought, if they are talking about corrective structures it could be more 
than twenty! 
I have seen some Elliotticians end a triple three with an expanded flat - which 
is something I would never even consider as a solution because the expanded flat 
is a 3-3-5 development with the potential for the middle "3" to be a complex 
correction or constructed of a double zigzag or triple three and thus implies a 
change to the way R.N. Elliott perceived corrective structures. I have not found 
any reason to alter his corrective structures and consider his findings as brilliant. 
I've been told that some end a triple three correction with a triangle. This latter 
"solution" implies that the next trend will begin from a level above/below the 
actual low/high (depending on the direction.) To be honest, I find this to be totally 
absurd to me, and smacks of trying to force a count. However, it can be a 


=== PAGE 45 ===

40 i DEFINING WAVES 
convenient method of trying to make sense of the following wave development so 
that the Elliottician can make sense of the first legs of the count he wanted. By 
that definition, they have basically declared that Elliott Wave is retrospective 
rather than predictive. 
In fact, all these hybrid add-ons, to try and resolve the count they wish to 
make, can be accommodated in the Harmonic Elliott Wave structure. First, let me 
provide a few examples of different traditional Elliott Wave counts and the 
varying projection ratios in Wave 3.

Fig 3-4 is a chart I that was passed on to me. The analyst had obviously been 
very bearish because he labeled the 2009 low in the Dow Jones Industrial Index 
as a Wave (1) and therefore he was looking for a Wave (2). Note the 5-wave

analyst had clearly decided that the 38.2o/o projection in the Wave C was enough 
and that because it coincided with the 5/8th retracement in Wave (2) to suggest 
that the Wave (3) was to commence. We all know that this did not occur with 
price very quickly resuming the uptrend. 
Note the two Wave 3's were around 1 09%- 1 1 0%. Also note the Wave 2 and 
Wave 4 in the Wave A were around 33.3% each. Clearly this offered no 
alternation in terms of depth or complexity. 
Particularly in the second two charts the counts are very different. Across all 
four charts shown above, the Wave 3 targets were: 
7 6.4%, 80%, 1 09%, 1 1 0%, 1 16%, 1 4 1  o/o, 1 45% and 1 60% 
This range of Wave 3 projection targets (in just 4 charts - which suggests there 
will be all variety of different projection ratios in other charts) begs the question: 
"How do we know where a wave will finish?" 
In some cases the labeling is either fixed to swing highs and swing lows or 
arbitrarily what the analyst thinks is best. As seen in Fig 3 - 1 ,  one analyst 
organized the correction to end higher than the lowest corrective low to ensure he 
could generate a 5-wave count. Obviously, this would have been decided only in 


=== PAGE 48 ===

retrospect. How do they select the high/low that should be labeled? Are they 
logical? So what constitutes a wave? How can we define a wave? Let's explore 
this a little further. 
There are three issues that I'd like to highlight in terms of identifying waves: 
-
That whether within Elliott's 5-wave structure, or my 3-wave structure, the 
lower degree waves must develop in accordance to the underlying theory of 
construction 
-
We need also understand the issue that lower degree wave counts and 
higher degree wave counts must match 
-
Finally, how do we know where the wave will stall if there are no 
consistent ratios? 
Let's review the first issue, that the lower degree must produce a structure that is 
consistent with the expectations of the higher degree.

This highlights the requirement to observe, where possible, the lower degree 
wave development to ensure that it has an appropriate wave development. On too 
many occasions I see this type of lack of attention to detail. Traditional 
Elliotticians are generally addicted to select major swing highs and swing lows to 
identify a wave beginning or ending (but never knowing when they will end.) 
Of course, when analyzing a daily chart back over several years it will be 
difficult to be absolutely certain of the lower degree structure. However, the fact 
that you have been unable to quantify the count must be kept in mind. 
The above two issues are actually very similar. The third, "how do we know 
when a wave will stall?" is the billion Dollar question. Already, through the first 
few examples in this chapter, we have not resolved the total unknown outcome of 
how long is a Wave 3 and where will it stop? Will it extend? If an impulsive 
wave does extend, will it be the Wave 1 that will extend, the Wave 3 or even 
Wave 5? Will the Wave 5 fail? The examples of the Wave 3 projections highlight 
that it is almost certain to be an impossibility to judge the ending of a Wave. I can 
state that from plenty of experience in failing. Yes, we can use momentum 
conditions to highlight risk of reversal when divergences develop but it's still a 
vague concept within the traditional 5-wave Elliott Wave structure. 
THE HARMONIC ELLIOTT WAVE SOLUTION 
When I first sat down to write Harmonic Elliott Wave in 2010, I was convinced 
of the 3-wave approach. This came from extreme frustrations using traditional 
Elliott Wave even though, when I worked in the direct market for Barclays Bank, 
I was considered an expert. I wrote forecasts on both the Reuters and (then) Dow 
Jones Telerate platforms and had many "fans" who wrote to me. I taught 
technical analysis to bank traders in Asia. When I moved away from the direct 
market to work in Dow Jones Telerate I Kyodo News Markets in Tokyo I 
presented technical analysis seminars, including Elliott Wave, and then wrote my 
first book, Integrated Technical Analysis, which was published in 1 999. 
I felt I did a good job with Elliott Wave, that is, until I began my own 
forecasting service. The frustration of "missing out a wave" whether that be a 
sooner than expected reversal or an unexpected additional wave that I hadn't 
anticipated, was all too much. I could not make out any consistent manner of 


=== PAGE 51 ===

46 I DEFINING WAVES 
identifying wave projections. The standard 1 6 1 .8%, 261 .8% projections etc., just 
didn't happen that often. (In fact, to expect all Wave 3 's to be confined to a 
handful of projection ratios is clearly absurd.) So I decided to research to see if 
there were any more consistent ratios between different areas of the structure. 
This is when I noted 223.6% repeated quite frequently - but between 3-wave 
moves and not 5-wave. 
It was then I decided to write a book, that being Harmonic Elliott Wave. When 
I began writing, the structure was still quite vague to me until I began a section of 
the book when I knew I had to justify the 3-wave structure rather than the 5-wave. 
I generated wave counts in both traditional Elliott Wave and my 3-wave 
methodology to compare the forecasting success. It was only then, probably two 
months into the process of writing the book that I realized how the fractal 
structure worked. I remain to this day, totally amazed at what appears to be a 
natural law of development that is encompassed across all wave degrees of all 
time scales. What's more, the fact that world wars, central bank intervention, high 
frequency trading and political manipulation has not (and does not) have any 
impact on this ratio structure. 
I should add that I misjudged a few issues in Harmonic Elliott Wave and it was 
only later that I made further discoveries after writing the book. Therefore, in this 
book I shall update the process in a more thorough manner. 
Let's look at the Harmonic Elliott Wave structure again: 
Fig 3-8. A simple 5-wave rally in the Dow Jones Industrial Index 
Fig 3-8 is the same as Fig 1 -2 in Chapter 1 .  This rally, as it progressed, had a 
series of projection ratios that could be used as estimated targets and during the 
process of development could be fine-tuned to identify the wave endings. As 


=== PAGE 52 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 47 
mentioned in Chapter 1 ,  I had already forecast the approximate 1 2,800 projection 
target in Wave (iii) of the Dow Jones Industrial Index in my first book, including 
the subsequent 20% correction. There are techniques to estimate the Wave -iii­
and Wave -iv- targets in advance although they required ratification and 
confirmation as approached. 
It is ratios that are consistent across all markets and all time frames that 
generate a truly fractal nature of market development using a standard impulsive 
structure across all 5-wave moves (and the 5-waves developing in groups of 3-
waves.) 
FIBONACCI PROJECTION AND RETRACEMENT RATIOS 
There is clearly a consistent set of ratio clusters for projections and for this I do 
use the Fibonacci Sequence. However, the projection ratios, particularly for Wave 
(iii), have no real similarities to those current! used. First, let me just go through 
the basics of the Fibonacci sequence and resultant ratio percentages. 
Leonardo Pisano Bogollo, (c. 1 170 
- c. 1 250) also known as Leonardo 
Fibonacci was an Italian mathematician is considered by many to have been one 
of the most talented western mathematicians of the Middle Ages. As a boy he 
studied the Hindu-Arabic numeral system which was easier to use than Roman 
numerals. 
In 1 202 he published his findings in his book, Liber Abaci, and introduced the 
number sequence that was known to Indian mathematicians as early as the 6th 
century. Later it became known as the Fibonacci Sequence. 
In the Fibonacci sequence of numbers, each number is the sum of the previous 
two numbers, starting with 0 and 1 .  Thus the sequence begins: 
0, 1 ,  1 ,  2, 3, 5, 8, 13, 2 1 ,  34, 55, 89, 1 44, 233, 377, 610 etc 
At first glance it seems an innocuous sequence but as we will see it holds quite 
extraordinary properties. By dividing one number in the sequence by the next the 
following occurs: 
Note that the first few numbers begin with common fractions: 
1 
1/2 
2/3 
3/5 
However, after 10 numbers we can see that the result remains at 61 .80%. This 
is the Golden Ratio which is found in nature (the coil of a snail's shell for 


=== PAGE 53 ===

48 I DEFINING WAVES 
example and even in the growth of the salmon population), in classical 
architecture including the Egyptian pyramids and some Greek structures such as 
the Acropolis and Parthenon. 
This method of generating ratios can be developed further, by dividing 
numbers that are two apart, three apart and so on in the sequence. Thus ratios a 
series of ratios can be generated below 100%. This is shown below in Fig 3.9. 
THE SQUARE ROOT OF TWO 
The square root of 2, also known as Pythagoras' constant, is the positive real 
number that, when multiplied by itself, gives the number 2. Geometrically the 
square root of 2 is the length of a diagonal across a square with sides of one unit 
of length; this follows from the Pythagorean theorem. It was probably the first 
number known to be irrational. Its numerical value truncated to 5 decimal places 
is: 1 .4 1421 
In mathematics, an irrational number is any real number that is not a rational 
number - that is, it is a number which cannot be expressed as a fraction x/y, where 
x and y are integers, with y being non-zero. Basically, this means numbers that it 
cannot be represented as a simple fraction. It can be proven that irrational 
numbers are precisely those real numbers that cannot be represented as 
terminating or repeating decimals, although mathematicians do not take that to be 
the definition. 
I do not profess to be a skilled mathematician and will go no further than this 
brief explanation. I was introduced to the use of the square root of two by an 
acquaintance in the market who described the ratio as commonly occurring within 
musical notes. 
At first I wasn't quite sure how to use this until I began to sit down and study 
wave relationships and noted that two derivations of the number frequently 
occurred: 4 1 .4% and it's "opposite" 58.6% being 1 00 - 41 .4. These ratios tend to 
appear most often in corrections. 
THE WAVE RELATIONSHIP MATRIX 
There are two ratio sets, one for the projection ratios and one for the 
retracement ratios. The core ratios below 1 00% are used both for corrections and 
projections - but clearly using a different process. The ratios are derived from the 
both the Fibonacci sequence and the square root of 2.

Fig 3-9. The source of ratios below 1 00% derived from the Fibonacci sequence

RETRACEMENT RATIOS 
Fig 3-9 displays all the Fibonacci derived ratios below 1 00%. In addition to the 
well known ratios of 23 .6%, 38.2%, 6 1 .8% & 76.4% the Fibonacci sequence also 
produces 85.4%, 9 1%, 9 1 .4% and several others but which are all quite close to 
each other. In addition there are the lower ratios generated by the reciprocals such 
as 9%, 5.6% and below. To these we can add 33.3, 50o/o, 66.7% together with the 
ratios derived from the square root of2: 4 1 .4% and 58.6%. 
Any of these ratios can be seen in retracements. 
It was only when observing some of the retracements - and even projections - in 
my research into common wave relationships, I noted that projections are very 
simple to calculate. It is simply that we add the ratios shown above to 1 00%, 
200%, 300% etc. However, there are two types of projections: those that are 
Wave (c) projections, Wave (iii) projections and Wave (v) projections. These 
work quite simply: 


=== PAGE 55 ===

50 I DEFINING WAVES 
Wave (c) projections 
Below 1 00%: 
6 1 .8%, 66.7%, 76.4%, 85.4%, 9 1 %, 94.4%, 98.7% 
Above 1 00%: 
1 02. 1 %, 1 05.6%, 1 09%, 1 14.6%, 1 23.6%, 1 38.2%, 141 .4%, 1 50%, 1 58.6%, 
1 6 1 .8%, 1 76.4%, 1 85.4%, etc. 
Note: Wave (c) projections are subservient to Wave (iii) projections and can less 
accurate 
Wave (v) projections 
30%, 33.3%, 38.2%, 4 1 .4%, 50%, 58.6%, 6 1 .8%, 66.7%, 76.4%, 85.4% 
Note: Wave (v) projections may stall between ratio projections. 
Wave (iii) projections 
I have left the Wave (iii) projections for last because there are very clear 
projection clusters and not all the sub-1 00% ratios are used. First, the minimum 
Wave (iii) is 1 76.4% although on rare occasions they can fall between 1 72%-
1 76.4%. 
The projection clusters are: 
1 76.4% - 198.7% 
223.6%, 238.2% and 261 .8o/o - 398.7% 
323.6%, 338.2% and 361 .8% - 398.7% 
423.6%, 438.2% and 461 .8o/o - 498.7% 
etc. 
With these ratios we can begin to build a structure with them. 
Wave (c) = Length of Wave (a) multiplied by a valid Wave (c) ratio projected 
from the Wave (b): 
e.g. Length of Wave (a) x 1 05.6% 


=== PAGE 56 ===

DEFINING WAVE$ I 51 
Wave (iii) = Length of Wave (i) multiplied by a valid Wave (iii) ratio projected 
from the Wave (ii): 
e.g. Length of Wave (i) x 198.7o/o 
Wave (v) = Length of the start of Wave (i) to the end of Wave (iii) multiplied by 
a valid Wave (v) ratio projected from the Wave (iv): 
e.g. Length of Wave (i) -> (iii) x 50% 
The above is a very brief description of the process. However, if you consider 
the entire process within both (say) the "daily" wave degree and across the range 
of wave degrees in a fractal manner it becomes obvious that since Wave (iii) is 
constructed of an (a)(b)(c), that the Wave (c) of Wave (iii) must have the same 
target as Wave (iii). Furthermore, since Wave (c) is constructed of 5-waves then 
it will have a Wave v. Therefore, there are three projection targets that must point 
to the same target. 
Fig 3-1 0  (below) displays the same chart shown ofthe complex wave structure 
shown in Chapter 1 .  Rather than the traditional wave count in Fig 3-4 in which 
the analyst was looking for a Wave (2) at the 5/8th retracement of the decline from 
the 14, 1 98 high we can see how the Dow Index has rallied as expected as detailed 
in my first book on Harmonic Elliott Wave.

Fig 3-1 0. The complex impulsive structure in the Dow Jones Industrial Index 


=== PAGE 57 ===

52 I DEFINING WAVES 
Apart from the valid projection and correction ratios as described in the 
explanation there is one more feature I want to highlight. You may recall that in 
Fig 3-4, that the Wave 2 and Wave 4 were both 33.3% and displayed no evidence 
of alternation. R.N. Elliott suggested that alternation tends to be displayed in both 
depth and complexity. Clearly in the chart in Fig 3-4 there was no alternation in 
either depth or complexity. Now look at Fig 3 - 1 0  again. Wave (ii) was relatively 
shallow and brief while Wave (iv) was both deep and complex. This is an almost 
1 00% reliable feature in Harmonic Elliott Wave although there are some 
exceptions. 
As a generally reliable guideline, there is normally a ratio relationship between 
Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) but can tend to shift depending on whether the 5-wave 
impulsive wave is situated in an underlying trending move or in a corrective 
move. I have tended to find that the sum of the ratios of Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) 
will be between 80% and 1 00% in a trending move while within a corrective 
structure there is more risk of the sum being between 1 00% and 1 20%. One 
exception can be in the case of a deep Wave (ii) of over 90%. It is possible that 
the Wave (iv) will be between 1 4.6%-23.6% but there are probably more 
examples of the Wave (iv) being as deep as 38.2% and in corrective structures up 
to 50%. These situations need to be observed with care. In equity markets I tend 
to find more instances when the sum of Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) are above 100%. 
As a fuller demonstration of the manner on which waves develop with the ratio 
structure described above, how lower degree projection ratios move in harmony 
with the higher degree and the incredible beauty of alternation throughout I shall 
provide the example of the development of the S&P Index. 
Fig 3-1 1 .  The construction of the Wave (1) in the S&P Index 


=== PAGE 58 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 53 
The first chart will be the equivalent seen in Fig 3-4 and how the Wave 1 ,  
which was also my Wave (i) but in a 3-wave development and not a 5-wave. 
When I began trying to work out whether this rally would develop in 5-waves 
in Wave ( 1 ), I was doubtful. As it happens, I managed to generate a count that 
seems to work. It took some ingenuity (if only because I have forgotten how to 
use the traditional 5-wave structure) but with a combination of diagonal triangles 
and ignoring any sense of alternation and just reacting to swing highs and lows, I 
made it work. 
When I look at this and wonder whether I would have been able to forecast any 
of the wave targets, I must say I think it would have been impossible. The 
structure is haphazard, displays no sense of alternation and no consistent ratios 
with any of the legs. I can just imagine those five Elliotticians sitting in a room 
making up all sorts of wave counts! 
Now look at the Harmonic Elliott Wave structure of exactly the same rally in 
Fig 3-12. I had never actually gone through this process in the Wave (i) before. 
At the time it was developing I was still a traditional Elliottician, was very 
bearish, looking for a bearish count - and rabidly bearish like everyone else. As I 
went through this I smiled to myself seeing the same sense of beauty I have 
become familiar with. 
There were two Wave -iii-'s, the first was just a few points below a 461 .8% 
projection and the other a few points below a 1 76.4% projection. Both Wave -v-'s 
were 50%. The Wave -c-'s were 1 50% and 1 66.7% respectively.

Now look at the alternation. The sum of Wave -ii- and Wave -iv- in Wave (a) 
was 1 12%. The sum of Wave -ii- and Wave -iv- in Wave (c) was 91%. The Wave 
(c) projection was just a little above 1 05.6%. While there are lower degree waves 
that I have not labeled, when you look at them you will see the same 
shallow/deep combination. 
Fig 3-13. The next higher degree in the S&P Index moving towards the Wave (iii) 
Fig 3-13 displays the daily chart following the Wave (i) and Wave (ii) through 
to the Wave (a) and Wave (b) of Wave (iii), followed by the decline in Wave (iv). 
The same structure of ratios is present along with alternation. The Wave -v- was a 
mere 38.2% to reach slightly below 1 76.4% projection in Wave (iii) and just over 
a 1 09% projection in Wave (c). The sum of the Wave -ii- and Wave -iv- ratios 
was 107.4%. The Wave (iv) was another forecast I made on the way down 
suggesting it should stall closer to the maximum 58.6% ending with a margin of 
1 .23 points. This was forecast just over one month in advance of the final low and 
can be seen and heard in the introductory recording that is at the top right of my 
blog. 
Just to press the point about the wave relationships and alternation, I will add 
the lower degree charts ( 60 minute) with ratios and alternation in the Wave -a­
and Wave -c- in the S&P's rally that was shown in Fig 3-13.

In the same manner, the Wave -a- ofWave -iii- seen in Fig 3-13 maintained the

not added the ratios for the Wave [a] I [iii] and Wave [c] I [v] but it is very clear 
that they both held to alternation, in particular the minor Wave ii in Wave [ c] 
provoked a long and deep correction in Wave iv as would be expected m 
alternation. 
This method of development is consistent across all wave degrees.

56 I DEFINING WAVES 
Fig 3- 1 5  displays the follow-through from the Wave -b- following the rally in 
Wave -a-, seen in Fig 3-14. It can be seen that, not only the structure in Wave -c­
/ -iii- held the normally seen ratios but also those of the lower degrees in the 
Wave [a] and Wave [c] of Wave [iii] and the Wave [c] of Wave [v]. (The hourly 
chart does not provide the key levels that are seen in the 5-minute chart.) 
Thus, from Fig 3-10 that followed the weekly development of the same rally in 
the Dow Jones Industrial Index, down through the daily chart and then to the 
hourly charts, all maintain the ratios as set out in the earlier explanation. I have 
taken this down to the 1 -minute chart and observed the same ratios and 
development. It can even be seen in the tick charts. However, when getting down 
to the 1 -minute chart and below any frenetic development following economic 
releases can cause a good deal of chaos. 
Equally, the correct relationships and complexity between Wave (ii) and Wave 
(iv) is consistent throughout the wave degrees. I am constantly stunned into 
wonderment as I see this pattern of ratios and alternation developing. 
Exceptions in Alternation 
While I have stated above that alternation is consistent, and is in the vast 
majority of cases, there are exceptions. While not in 1 00% of cases, if the Wave 
(b) of Wave (iii) is very deep (over 66.7% and perhaps up to 85.4% - or a little 
more) the Wave (iv) can occasionally be shallower than expected causing the sum 
of Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) to be less than 80%. I am so accustomed to alternation 
working that a shortfall of 80% tends to take me by surprise, but it is useful to 
keep this potential exception in mind. 
An alternative event that can also "compensate" for alternation is in the Wave 
(b) of Wave (v). I have found this more in the U.S. equity indices. On some 
occasions (particularly in the NASDAQ) should the Wave (iv) fall short, the 
Wave (b) of Wave (v) can be very deep. Therefore, if the sum of Wave (ii) and 
Wave (iv) fall below (say) 75% then consider the possibility of a deep Wave (b) 
of Wave (v). 
In general, I consider the depth in alternation a guideline rather than a rule but 
it is consistent enough that, while I have not collected any statistical evidence, I 
estimate that it occurs 90%-95% of the time. 
A final note on alternation, which I mentioned earlier but will repeat in this 
section again, is that should the Wave (ii) retrace of 90% there is a greater risk of 
a Wave (iv) that could be more than 23.6o/o. It tends to happen more in particular 
markets: currency crosses, GBPUSD has that tendency but overall it is wise to 
observe the lower degree development to judge the corrective structure, whether 


=== PAGE 62 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 57 
it is a complete triple three, or where the break of a key swing level can provoke 
resumption of the impulsive move in Wave (a) ofWave (v). 
IDENTIFYING PROJECTION TARGETS 
One of the automatic questions thrown at me is "there are so many projection 
targets - how do I know which will be hit?" 
It's an obvious and necessary question. There are two basic alternative 
methods depending on which two waves need to match or whether there is no 
target of which we can be certain. In this chapter I will describe the process of 
forecasting within an impulsive structure. 
For this, you can download a spreadsheet from my website that aids the 
process by being able to follow a series of impulsive waves across the wave 
degrees. I shall include some images of the spreadsheet and demonstrate the 
methodology. As this process develops, it will naturally bring into the process the 
requirement to think about where waves targets are likely to develop and also 
how to follow the lower degree development to confirm that it is moving in line 
with expectations. During this development the characteristics of different waves 
will become apparent although I will examine the other corrective waves and also 
Wave (a)'s in Chapter 4. 
This methodology of matching wave targets can be made throughout the wave 
degrees from the 1 -minute chart through monthly.

Fig 3-1 7. The spreadsheet with the individual wave values inserted 
As can be seen, values are inserted into the shaded blocks at the top of the 
spreadsheet. These provide the individual ratios for each wave. While, before 
Harmonic Elliott Wave, I hadn't touched equity markets at all, even after the 
Wave (ii) the 1 98.7% projection in Wave (iii) would have been a prospective 
target. Why is that? The answer is that this is the Wave ((A)) of Wave ((V)) and it 
is common for them to reach to just below or just above the Wave ((III)) which 
was at 14,198.1 0. This provides a handy method of estimating the Wave (iii) 
target. Because we need the Wave (v) to stall around the 14,198. 10 high Gust 
below or above) it is possible to test alternatives by inserting a valid projection 
ratio. In this way, with the knowledge that Wave (ii) being just under 33.3%, we 
would know that the Wave (iv) would be at least 50%. Therefore, using the 
spreadsheet we could not only ensure that the Wave (iv) would remain above the 
Wave (b) of Wave (iii), but also judge whether the Wave (v) would generate an 
appropriate target close to 1 4, 1 98. 1 0. This was exactly the process I used when 
making the forecast in my book, Harmonic Elliott Wave, to estimate the Wave 
(iii). However, another alternative would have been to consider the 1 76.4% 
projection at 1 2,335.02.

Fig 3-19. How the lower degree targets match the higher degree 


=== PAGE 65 ===

60 I DEFINING WAVES 
Fig 3-19 displays two levels on the spreadsheet. The lower spreadsheet 
contains the higher degree (weekly) levels for each wave while the higher is for 
the Wave (c) of Wave (iii). Note that the higher spreadsheet begins from the 
Wave (b) level seen in the lower spreadsheet - 9,614.32. Through this process, 
once the Wave -iii- and Wave -iv- have been confirmed we can make sure that 
the Wave -v- will reach a valid Wave (iii) projection target. The Wave -v- was 
not perfect (at 47.5%) but as earlier mentioned, the Wave -c- I -v- targets are 
subservient to the Wave (iii) targets. 
Had the Wave -c- of Wave -iii- developed with a stronger rally, then it would 
have been necessary to push the Wave (iii) target higher, perhaps to the 223.6% 
or 238.2o/o. This is something we have to be aware of as the wave develops and 
make adjustments in response. 
Thus, the forecast for Wave (iii) in the first book on Harmonic Elliott Wave 
was perfect together with the forecast correction of around 50o/o of the rally from 
Wave (ii). 
This methodology is also useful in other circumstances, as I'll 
describe later. 
CORRECTIVE WAVES 
This may seem to be a relatively simple process of using the retracement ratios 
from 5.6% through to 1 00%. In terms of Wave (b), Wave (x) and Wave (iv) that 
is basically the case. However, corrections come in all different shapes and sizes 
so a little more attention must be given to these unwelcome complications. In 
Harmonic Elliott Wave there are also Wave (b)'s throughout the impulsive 
structure that need a slightly different approach. In a Wave (iii) the Wave (b) 
tends to have a maximum retracement of 76.4%-85.4% but there are rare 
instances where I have seen 90o/o and just a little more. These need additional 
care. Indeed, I tend to consider the Wave (b) of Wave (iii) as a flexible buffer 
region which can absorb price shocks and I or uncertainty. 
Wave (b) 
Retracements between 5.6o/o (rare) and 1 00% are possible. 
Wave (b)'s are seen in all structures - both impulsive and corrective structures. 
Within corrective structures, while they can be constructed of all combination of 
simple and/or complex structures they can be extremely difficult to follow. Even 
in Wave (ii) or Wave (iv), there can also be complications but tend to be less than 
in corrective structures. 


=== PAGE 66 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 61 
Fig 3-20. Examples of Wave (b) corrections in different positions
