Fig 3-20 displays several examples of Wave (b) corrections in different 
positions. These range from an almost minimum 1 7.4% in the hourly decline in 
AUDUSD on the left to a 77.6% retracement in Wave b of Wave iii in the same 
chart, while in the chart to the right, in daily S&P Index, there is a 93.4% 
correction in Wave b of Wave iii. In total there are ten Wave B retracements 
across the two charts that represent a typical range of ratios. 
This may cause a sense of dismay in terms of not really knowing how deep 
these corrections can retrace but firstly, this is something we have to accept, and 
secondly, this is where there is a need for indicators to help us judge the potential 
support or resistance areas. The mere fact that we know there is a wide range of 
retracement ratios provides us with a sense of caution. Equally when these appear 
in daily charts we need to observe the hourly and possibly even 5-minute charts to 
follow the development of these corrections. If in the hourly charts, then 
observing the 5-minute charts is required. 
Wave (x) 
A Wave (x) retracement is a correction to the prior (a)(b)(c) move. It has a 
minimum retracement of 5.6%, although I rarely see much below 14.6%. The 
maximum retracement normally remains above/below the prior Wave (b) but not 
in 1 00% of the cases. I have notices that these breaches of the prior Wave (b) 
normally develop following the first zigzag. Where a Wave (b) develops as a 
triangle it is possible for the Wave (x) to deepen beyond the prior Wave (b), that 
is, the end of the triangle and towards the Wave AD or Wave 1\ A. In a triple three, 
the second Wave (x) will not penetrate the first Wave (x). 


=== PAGE 67 ===

62 I DEFINING WAVES 
Fig 3-2 1 .  Examples ofWave (x) corrections in the weekly GBPUSD chart 
Fig 3-2 1  displays a complicated development in GBPUSD in which there was 
a Wave (b) correction that developed as a triple three within a larger bullish 
correction - and that appears to be developing as a double zigzag. With the Wave 
(b) being within a larger corrective structure, it is perhaps, not too surprising to 
see the depth of the Wave -x-'s being very deep although I would not suggest that 
is the norm. However, suffice to say that such complicated corrective structures 
can be quite frustrating and subject to errors. 
I have covered the subject of Wave (iv)'s within the description of the 
impulsive wave structure and will not add anything further at this point. Later on 
I shall bring all these factors together. 
Flat Corrections 
Flat corrections can develop in any corrective position: Wave (ii), Wave (iv), 
Wave (b) or Wave (x). The depth should be relevant for that position although in 
Wave (b) of Wave (iii) it is rare to see a deep correction. The first leg, the Wave 
FA (Flat Wave A) will always develop in 3-waves as described in Chapter Two. I 
shall not therefore repeat the explanation but merely provide an example of 
another flat correction. 
Fig 3-22 displays a Wave (iv) correction in the hourly NASDAQ chart which 
was formed with a single zigzag, the Wave -b- of which developed as a flat 
correction. The retracement in Wave FC, in the majority of cases, doesn't extend 


=== PAGE 68 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 63 
much beyond the Wave FA. In this example it became a little deeper than normal. 
In general the Wave FC should be measured by the retracement of the move from 
the end of the trending move to the Wave -a-.

Expanded Flats 
As with flat corrections, expanded flats can develop in any corrective position: 
Wave (ii), Wave (iv), Wave (b) or Wave (x). The depth should be relevant for 
that position although in Wave (b) of Wave (iii) it is rare to see a deep correction. 
The first leg, the Wave EFA (Expanded Flat Wave A) will always develop in 3-
waves as described in Chapter Two. Once again, I shall not therefore repeat the 
explanation but merely provide an example of another flat correction. 
Fig 3-23 (below) displays a Wave (iv) correction in the daily Hang Seng Index 
chart within zigzag, the Wave -b- of which developed as a flat correction. The 
retracement in Wave EFC, in the majority of cases, doesn't extend much beyond 
the Wave EF A. In general the Wave EFC should be measured by the retracement 
of the move from the end of the trending move to the Wave -a-. 
The expansion in Wave EFB normally ranges from around 1 4.6% to 38.2% of 
the depth of Wave EF A from the end of the directional wave in liquid markets. 
Occasionally I do see expansions up to 50% that can occur on occasions in equity 
markets and less liquid markets, or cross rates. 


=== PAGE 69 ===

64 I DEFINING WAVES 
Fig 3-23. An expanded flat correction in Wave -b- of a decline in Wave (b) in the 
Hang Seng Index 
Triangles 
Triangles only appear in Wave (b), Wave (x) and Wave (iv). They do not 
develop in Wave (ii). The majority of triangles develop within converging lines 
but on occasions one leg can be irregular rather like an expanded flat. However, 
the maximum expansion tends to be 38.2%. The first leg in Wave AA will always 
be in 3-waves as will the last leg, Wave /\E. Wave AB, Wave /\C and Wave AD 
can be in any combination of zigzags with potential for the Wave b' s and Wave 
x's to be in complex corrections. 
Wave /\A 
A retracement relevant to the wave being retraced. 
Wave /\B 
Mostly 76.4% - 85.4% in a retracement to Wave /\A 
Wave AC 
Mostly 66.7% - 76.4% ofWave AA 
Wave AD 
Mostly 66.7o/o - 76.4% of Wave /\B 
Wave AE 
Mostly 50.0% - 66.7% ofWave AC 
The ratios above cover the majority of triangles but there are exceptions. 
Occasionally, all legs can be up to 85.4%. On occasions where an irregular 
triangle develops the leg after the expansion will be a much higher projection, 


=== PAGE 70 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 65 
perhaps 85.4% to 1 05.6%. Therefore, where possible, it is important to follow the 
development of the internal structure. 
In Fig 24 a bearish triangle developed in Gold. Note the 80% retracement in 
Wave AB followed by around 66.7% in the following legs. Normally, I tend to see 
the Wave A A developing more directly and this larger Wave A A structure is 
unusual.

detail in respect of where they may stall.

WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
In this chapter I shall try and provide some guidelines to the characteristics of 
each wave. Within the range of waves, there are some that have multiple wave 
degree targets that need to converge towards target areas. However, there are 
others that have no strict targets. However, before I describe each wave 
individually, I'd like to just cover the topic of identification of support and 
resistances because these can assist in the process. 
The Harmonic Elliott Wave structure, as well as providing a measurable 
structure, also has many logical and recognizable characteristics to each wave and 
also natural stalling points or targets. These are predominantly derived from R.N. 
Elliott's guidelines and where I have modified the 5-wave development into a 3-
wave development, the equivalent guideline that Elliott offered. I shall also 
describe some alternative methods of forecasting support and resistance that can 
be helpful in determining either temporary stalling points or potential targets. 
First, let's just take a second look at the guidelines from Chapter One that are 
relevant to Harmonic Elliott Wave and add one more: 


=== PAGE 73 ===

68 I WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
• If Wave 2 is complex then Wave 4 will be simple (and vice versa) 
• Following a Wave B in a corrective ABC structure price will retest the 
prior Wave B 
• After completion of a Wave (v) price will return to the span of the prior 
Wave (b) ofWave (v) 
• After a Wave ( v) the first or second reversal will move to the prior swing 
high or swing low, quite often the Wave (iv) 
I have covered the topic of alternation quite thoroughly in Chapter three. 
Therefore, initially I will focus on the other two, together with another two being 
the reversal to a prior swing high or swing low, quite often the Wave (iv) and the 
reversal higher to a prior low point, or reversal lower to a prior high point.

Fig 4-1 .  Examples of natural targets on reversals in the daily Hang Seng Index 
Fig 4-1 contains nine examples of natural targets in the Hang Seng Index that 
are seen soon after a reversal: 
1 .  Following the completion of Wave -a- the first reversal in Wave i 
reached the area of the first corrective low in the prior rally 
2. Following the Wave ii correction we then saw Wave a stall in the span 
ofthe Wave [b] ofWave [v] 
3. The completed 5-wave decline in Wave (a) stalled just above the Wave 
[iv] swing low 


=== PAGE 74 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 69 
4. On the reversal higher from Wave (a) the Wave a stalled closer to the 
high of the prior Wave b of Wave v 
5. Following the Wave (c) of -b- low the first reversal in Wave [i] stalled 
just below the prior Wave iv. (This is common when the Wave b of 
Wave v is very shallow) 
6. As the rally extended, the Wave iii stalled just below the high of the 
prior Wave (b) 
7. The first reversal from the Wave [a] high stalled just above the prior 
corrective low 
8. The follow-through in Wave -c- stalled just above the prior Wave iv low 
9. After the subsequent Wave -a- low the correction in Wave -b- stalled 
just below the Wave -x- and in the congestion area 
This type of behavior is very consistent and can help us in identifying potential 
stalling points that can highlight where particular waves may stall.

INDIVIDUAL WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
In Chapter Three I described the basic process of forecasting the impulsive 
structure and in particular the Wave (c) I (iii) process, the Wave (iv) and Wave 
(v). Within that structure I also noted how to estimate the lower degree 
development required to ensure the Wave -v- of Wave (c) would reach the Wave 
(iii) target and the process of adjustment by exploring the alternative projections. 
However, there will always be challenges, particularly within corrective 
patterns and how they slot into the structures, that I shall try and provide some 
guidelines for each wave to offer a little more clarity to the analysis. 
Wave (a) 
If this is a Wave (a) of Wave (i) in a daily chart then again we should be 
watching the development of the five waves that construct Wave (a). As 
discussed above, because this is a new directional wave in a Wave (i), (iii), (v) or 
even within a multiple ABC correction, we shall need to be aware of the normal 
natural targets indicated by the development to the prior Wave -v-. 
Wave (a) of (i) targets could include: 
-
The return to Wave -b- of Wave -v-
-
The return to the prior Wave -iv-
-
A 
prior 
support/resistance 
area 
that 
may 
naturally 
provide 
resistance/support respectively 
-
The return into a triangle consolidation, if applicable 
-
The return back into a significant consolidation range 
Of course, in the process, the development must be in 5-waves that can be 
inserted into the spreadsheet. Occasionally, there can be problems in identifying 
the internal waves if this has begun in the (say) 5-minute chart when either the 
reversal is so sharp, or conversely when there has been a significant amount of 
erratic noise in the move that has many swings. This event will require some care 


=== PAGE 77 ===

72 ɥ WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
to be taken to confirm the reversal, possibly by a break above/below the prior 
swing high/low respectively. 
Refer to Fig 4-1 to Fig 4-3 for examples of natural support or resistance areas. 
We should also be referring to other technical indicators. In terms of noting 
momentum conditions and on occasions an alternative area marked by an 
indicator such as my Price Equilibrium Cloud.

Fig 4-4. How indicators can provide support or resistance on the first reversal 
While the price structure does contain natural support and resistance levels it is 
also possible to use indicators that are overlaid on price to indicate areas of 
support or resistance. In this example, in Fig 4-4, I have used my Price 
Equilibrium Clouds in the higher timeframe ( 4-hour) chart to provide initial 
resistance for the recovery from Wave [b] in the hourly chart. In a later chapter I 
shall discuss the benefits of indicators and multiple time frame charts. 
Wave (b) 
These waves are the wild card in the pack although there are different risks in 
each of the waves in which it divides. Wave (b) is seen in: 
-
Impulsive: Wave (i), Wave (iii) or Wave (v) 
-
Corrective: Wave (b) within a Zigzag, Double Zigzag or Triple Three 
-
Complex corrections: Wave (b) can appear in any of the elements in all 
three complex corrections 


=== PAGE 78 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 73 
In normal circumstances we can begin by looking for possible stalling points 
at: 
-
The extreme of Wave -iv- (normally a prominent swing high/low) 
-
A retracement into the span of a prior Wave (x) 
-
Potential for Fibonacci retracement ratios 
-
The development of the lower degree structure to identify ABC moves 
-
A return to a prior support/resistance in a correction lower/higher 
respectively 
There are many amazing features of Harmonic Elliott Wave that truly have me 
astounded. However, I have to admit that there is one wave that can drive me 
insane. That's the Wave (b). 
In many ways they are the cushions that absorbs the market's uncertainties at 
times when it is uncertain of the direction. It allows for an extremely shallow 
retracements but also very deep. Obviously, as mentioned above, working within 
a 3-wave structure there are Wave (b)'s everywhere - in Wave (i), (iii) and Wave 
(v), in the impulsive sequence and in all corrective structures. I think that anyone 
who uses Elliott Wave will be used to the Wave (b) within corrective structures 
but we need to address the Wave (b) characteristics within an impulsive wave. 
If I am to equate the Wave (b) of Wave (iii) to traditional Elliott Wave, they 
are most likely to be treated as being one of the extended waves within an 
impulsive move or even mistaken, because of the erratic development, for 
corrective waves. Here is just one example in EURUSD:

Fig 4-5. A 5-wave rally with multiple deep Wave b of Wave iii's

74 1 WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
Fig 4-5 displays a perfect rally in EURUSD but had a large number of deep 
Wave b of Wave iii's. From the left there was a 79% retracement in the first 
Wave b of Wave iii. From that Wave b low - in the next Wave c of Wave iii - the 
lower degree Wave b of Wave iii was 80%. Once reaching the Wave [a] high 
there was a Wave [b] of 66.7%. In the rally in Wave [c] there was a Wave b of 
Wave iii of 6 1 .8% and within the Wave c of Wave iii the Wave b of Wave iii was 
80%. 
I can understand why traditional Elliotticians were considering this as 
corrective, but this example does demonstrate the function that Wave b's provide. 
Very clearly we cannot expect the market to adhere to a limited range of 
projection/retracement ratios and the Wave b therefore acts as a "catch-all" that 
can absorb market events and still retain an impulsive structure. Indeed, in this 
development, soon after the Wave [a] was seen I forecast the Wave [iii] and 
Wave [iv] targets two weeks in advance. These two waves occurred over a Non­
Farm Payrolls release which is a testament to the potential accuracy using this 
technique. 
These are examples of deep Wave (b)'s. They can also be exceptionally 
shallow. A good example is one that occurred in the S&P Index back in October

((A)): 
These are just several examples of how Wave (b)'s can develop. Of course 
they can develop in Flat Corrections, Expanded Flat Corrections and Triangles 
also. These all provide the market with the chance to hold within corrective 
patterns until a stronger influence comes along.

Wave (ii) 
Wave (ii)'s are difficult to judge. The retracement can be as shallow as 1 4.6o/o 
but can be as much as 1 00%. This is rather like the "wild card" Wave (b) in that 
we never really know how deep the Wave (ii) will retrace. In normal 
circumstances we can begin by looking for: 
-
An initial reversal to the span of the prior Wave -b- of Wave -v-
-
The extreme of Wave -iv- (normally a swing high/low) 
-
A retracement into the span of the Wave (b) of Wave (i) 
-
Potential for Fibonacci retracement ratios 
-
The development of the lower degree structure to identify ABC moves

Fig 4-9 displays the correction from the Wave (i) rally seen in Fig 4-8. 
Following the completion of (c) of (i) the Wave a stalled, as expected, in the span 
of the prior Wave b of Wave v. Following the correction in Wave b there was a 
decline in Wave c which we would have had to follow in the lower degree charts 
to measure the 5-wave decline. The Wave c was a 93% projection and retraced 
30% from the Wave (i) high. 
With just one abc development we would have no information to suggest this 
was the completed correction. At this stage it would have been possible to see a 
Wave x followed by a second abc to form a double zigzag and even later for a 
triple three. Equally, another alternative at that time would be the potential for a 
complex correction, a flat correction or expanded flat. (Wave (ii) will never 


=== PAGE 83 ===

78 I WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
develop as a triangle.) Therefore, we could not really confirm resumption of gains 
in Wave (a) of (iii) until the 38.2%-50% expansion in a possible expanded flat 
had been broken. (This would allow the potential risk of a recycling lower back to 
the Wave c low, and probably just below.) However, on this occasion the rally 
did resume directly. 
It does highlight the challenge that needs to be faced in terms of a single zigzag 
correction and to a lesser extent a double zigzag correction in a Wave (ii). Of 
course, there could be a triple three. 
Fig 4-1 0. A double zigzag in a Wave [ii] position in the 5-min EURUSD chart 
Fig 4-1 0  is the 5-minute chart in EURUSD. In the lower degree charts the 
development can be noisy and even within one 5-minute bar there may be a need 
to drop down into the tick bar charts to observe the development to ensure that an 
impulsive structure has developed. Therefore, on initial observance the structure 
can be confusing. In these instances the first thing to do is work out where a valid 
5-wave move occurred. To the extreme left of the chart I could see the potential 
5-wave structure from where I have labeled Wave [b] and indeed, it measured 
well. I then went to the tick bar charts to ensure that the decline in Wave [a] was 
indeed in 5-waves. (A tick-bar is a bar constructed of a fixed number of ticks. In 
this case I used a bar comprised of 1 0  ticks.) 
The chart shows another correction in Wave [ii] that was formed of a double 
zigzag with each 5-wave leg holding a solid impulsive sequence. Since the double 
zigzag ended only a few points below the Wave -ii- (to the top left of the chart) I 
knew that a triple three would not develop and that the decline in Wave [a] would 
therefore develop.

Fig 4-1 1  displays a triple three decline in Wave [ii] in USDJPY. It was 
particularly complicated and this statement basically reflects the comments I 
made in Chapter Two concerning multiple ABC corrections. The structure has a 
wide swinging triangle in the first Wave -b- and an expanded flat in Wave -x­
(although this could only be seen in the 5-minute chart), following the first Wave 
-c-. The second Wave -b- developed as a large triangle although following the 
completion of this Wave -b- the following Wave -c-, Wave -x- and final ABC 
decline were relatively straightforward. Note that while the second Wave -x- did 
retrace above the previous Wave -b-, it did not break above the triangle high. 
I would not say that it would be easy to get all of these moves correct as it was 
being followed. There were quite a few traps and particularly, as the triangle 
began, we'd not really be thinking about a triangle but more of a second zigzag 
and would (and did) cause the risk of heading down blind alleys on occasions. 
Later I will add additional analysis that can provide more information from 
indicators that could provide more supporting evidence at anticipated reversal 
areas. 
Wave (x) 
A Wave (x) is basically the corrective wave between ABC moves in a double 
zigzag or triple three. They are similar to the Wave (b) in regard to the depth of 
the correction although in the vast majority of cases they will stall above/below 
the recent Wave (b) but can retrace deeper on occasions. 


=== PAGE 85 ===

80 I WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
A Wave (x) can develop as any of the basic corrective structures: 
-
A simple zigzag 
-
A double zigzag 
-
A triple three 
-
A flat correction 
-
An expanded flat correction 
-
A triangle 
As a guideline to the common natural stalling points, the following can be 
considered: 
(lv) 
(b) 
-
The extreme of Wave -iv- (normally a swing high/low) 
-
A retracement into the span of a prior Wave (b) 
-
Potential for Fibonacci retracement ratios, whether above or below the 
prior Wave (b) 
-
A return to a prior support/resistance in a correction lower/higher 
respectively

Fig 4-12. A complex corrective development in the weekly GBPUSD chart 
Fig 4-1 2  displays five occasions where Wave X develops in a weekly 
correction in GBPUSD. Within the decline from the first Wave (c) high - to the 
left of the chart the Wave (x) - developed in a triple three. This first Wave (x) 
stalls in the span of the prior Wave (b). The two instances of Wave x in the 


=== PAGE 86 ===

DEFINING WAVES I 81 
decline do not appear to have followed any particular natural support or 
resistance. Therefore, it would have been important for each wave a and Wave c 
to have been measured in the daily, perhaps hourly charts. 
From the Wave (x) low a 5-wave rally developed to form a new Wave (a). The 
Wave (b) then developed as a second triple three. It is noticeable that both 
instances in the Wave -x-, the retracement was above the prior Wave -b-. I 
suspect there may have been some Elliotticians who would count the structure in 
the second Wave -b- as an expanding triangle. However, this would be wrong 
because the rally to the peak marked as the second Wave -x- was a 5-wave 
structure. In terms of a pattern it could be considered an expanding triangle and 
thus used as a target, but very clearly it fell far too short of the implied target. 
Therefore, I feel justified in the count I have applied. 
Once again, each Wave -x- in the Wave (b) decline does not appear to have 
stalled at natural stalling points. 
In summary, Wave (x)'s can be particularly complicated because they are 
basically corrective structures within a larger corrective structure. They need to 
be navigated with care because of the propensity to generate complex corrections 
within an already complicated corrective development. Again, within the 
respective Wave A's and Wave B 's the focus on identifying the structure through 
the use of the spreadsheet is a necessity. 
Wave (iii) 
I have covered the methodology of forecasting Wave (iii) projections in 
Chapter Three. Obviously the Wave (c) target and Wave (iii) target must match. I 
shall not therefore repeat that process but merely point out some potential target 
areas and note the impact of the Wave (b) of Wave (iii) on the eventual target. At 
the very least, they should be considered in terms of natural support or resistance 
areas, but of course with a relevant Wave (iii) projection ratio. 
Potential target areas are: 
-
A prior Wave (b) - normally in the larger time frame/wave degree 
-
A prior major swing high/low 
-
If within a larger Wave (C) of Wave (III) it is possible to estimate the 
Wave (iii) of Wave C) in order that, following the Wave (iv) that the 
Wave (v) will reach the Wave (III) target 
-
Significant congestion areas

Fig 4-14 displays the first decline from the Wave (B) high in GBPUSD that

Wave (iv) 
As with Wave (iii), I have already described this wave in Chapter Three. In this 
section I shall merely highlight with a few more examples how the relationship in 
terms of alternation, both depth and complexity, with Wave (ii). This is normally 
a relatively straightforward wave to forecast except in the situations when Wave 
(ii) is particularly deep and above 90%. 
Wave (iv)'s can develop as: 
-
Zigzags 
-
Double zigzags 
-
Triple threes 
-
Flat corrections 
-
Expanded flat corrections 
-
Triangles 
NB. Wave (iv) cannot breach and normally does not reach the Wave (b) of 
Wave (iii). 
Refer to Chapter Two and Chapter Three for the descriptions and 
characteristics of corrections.

All four examples in Fig 4-1 6  display alternation although not all are between 
80% and 1 20% but do remember that the 80-120 area is a guideline and not a 
rule. When you go through a number of these relationships you will begin to 
notice that different markets have a tendency to produce varying relationships. In 
Forex, I tend to find that in a strong impulsive move the sum tends towards the 
80%-100% area while in corrections they can get above 1 00%. In U.S. equities I 
find the sum tends towards the 1 OOo/o-1 1  0% area. Some can get above 1 20%. 
There are also different ways in which alternation can be satisfied even when 
Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) remain below 80%: 
-
If Wave (ii) is above 90% there is a stronger risk that Wave (iv) could 
still be relatively deep, as much as 38.2% or 50% 
-
If Wave (b) of Wave (iii) was very deep (more than 66.7%) then the 
Wave (iv) could also be shallow 
-
If Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) are both shallow, then there is a risk that the 
Wave (b) ofWave (v) could be very deep 
These are frustrating alternatives - although the very deep Wave (ii) will 
always risk a Wave (iv) that could be deep, but it's not a rule. Indeed, the Wave 
(iv) could be shallow. Therefore, it is important to attempt to identify the lower 
degree corrective structure to see if there is a completed correction. Even then, 
there are so many corrective structures it can be quite tricky. 
If both Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) are shallow enough to see the sum below 80% 
the risk is that the Wave (iv) will be missed, which is quite frustrating because it's 
normally a pretty easy target to identify. However, it will warn of the potential for 
a deep Wave (b) of(v). 
In the case of a deep Wave (b) of Wave (iii), the potential for a shallower 
Wave (iv) will always be there. However, I don't find this occurs too frequently. 


=== PAGE 91 ===

86 I WAVE CHARACTERISTICS 
Wave (v) 
Normally, the range of the Wave (v) is relatively simple to identify as long as 
there is another higher degree target available - a Wave (III) for example. 
However, there will be many instances where the range of Wave (v) projections 
could reach two higher degree Wave (III) targets. (By this I mean that a 50% 
projection in Wave (v) could reach the 261 .8% projection in Wave (III) but a 
76.4% projection could reach the 298.7% projection in Wave (III).) In this case, it 
is important to follow the lower degree development of the Wave (a) and Wave 
(c) of Wave (v). In addition, while the majority of Wave (v)'s average around 
50%, I have seen them only manage to reach only around 23.6%, which only just 
breaks the high/low of the Wave (iii) and quickly reverses. Having said that, I 
don't find this happens with excessive frequency. 
Perhaps the most difficult Wave (v) to identify accurately is that in a Wave 
(A). Since the general projection targets can range from 33.3% to 85.4% there's 
quite a large expanse of target area. I did cover this earlier in the chapter by 
providing observations of common natural support or resistance areas that can be 
observed. Even then, there could still be a range of (say) 50% to 76.4% so the 
only real way is to work through the Wave (a), (b) and Wave (c) and observing 
the Wave v of Wave (c) targets compared to the Wave (v) targets. Of course, the 
Wave (v) targets needn't necessarily be nice clean ratios such as 50% or 61 .8%, 
66.7% and 76.4% (which I find relatively rare). Invariably the majority stall 
somewhere in between.

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
As I've worked with Harmonic Elliott Wave over the past 5 years it has given 
me plenty of experience with the methodology including problem periods. It's an 
absolutely stunning tool, but like any technique there will always be periods of 
difficulties, snags to overcome and market development that can be very 
confusing. Of course, at these times, it is necessary to employ a range of 
analytical techniques to cope with the market and reduce, or even square out a 
position. 
While I tend to use the 5-minute charts to generate my core views there are 
times when even this can be confusing. I see some traders/analysts preferring to 
analyze only from the hourly chart or higher. This is most certainly not preferred. 
Yes, looking back over significant periods of time will make the process of 
identifying precise lower degree waves impossible to identify, but in terms of 
what is happening now, it is the very basic platform on which to build wave 
degrees. 
In this chapter I shall try and outline some core techniques, hints, tips and 
solutions for your analysis. Remember, Harmonic Elliott Wave has a strong 


=== PAGE 94 ===

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES I 89 
foundation of logic which, with the use of the spreadsheet and the basic rules and 
guidelines, can make the solutions a lot easier, or at worst allow options that will 
point you back on the right track. This even applies to the periods where perhaps 
the daily development is in a complex corrective phase and you need to set 
parameters for various options. 
In this chapter I will focus on Harmonic Elliott Wave alone. Later I shall pull 
together other technical analysis methodologies that can help in understanding the 
underlying direction and even where indicators are in conflict with the wave 
count that you may have. At the core, if you have forecast a (say) Wave (iii) 
target you must have the indicators confirming a potential reversal at that target. 
If they suggest the trend isn't complete then you need to check for alternatives. I 
will also explain the use of multiple time-frame indications that can generate 
extremely useful information. 
ALTERNATION 
This is one of my favorite tools because, not only is it so reliable in the vast 
majority of situations but also let's you know that something is wrong if the 
relationship between Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) fails. Once you have seen how 
Wave (ii) has developed you have a strong idea of how the Wave (iv) must 
develop in both depth and complexity. Equally, when looking at a chart that 
appears to be a mess of swing highs and lows, alternation often provides the clue 
to identify the key structure. 
-
There can be several options for alternation: 
-
Wave (ii) is shallow and simple - thus Wave (iv) will likely be deep and 
complex 
-
Wave (ii) is deep and simple - thus Wave (iv) will likely be shallow and 
complex 
-
Wave (ii) is shallow and complex - thus Wave (iv) will likely be deep 
and simple 
-
Wave (ii) is deep and simple - thus Wave (iv) will likely be shallow and 
complex

Fig 5-1 .  Comparison of Harmonic Elliott Wave versus traditional Elliott Wave 
Fig 5-1 displays the same rally in the Dow Jones Industrial Index from the 
2009 low with the Harmonic Elliott Wave count on the left and a traditional 
Elliott Wave count that I sourced from the Internet, on the right. The traditional 
Elliott Wave count demonstrates its own weakness because there is never any 
certainty that the "normal" way of counting lacks clarity as to which swing highs 
or lows are the critical ones. 
There are obviously numerous occasions where there are impulsive wave 
counts. In the Harmonic count, it can be seen that the general pattern of 
alternation in terms of depth is present. There are one or two that do not quite fit 
the 80/120 guideline but during the process from July 201 0  I did note that the 
occasions where alternation fell a bit short, then the Wave b I iii or Wave b I v 
tended to compensate. 
In the chart on the right, the 2009 low was labeled Wave (A) following an 
expanded flat from the high in year 2000 (not pictured). (Once again, this was a 
random chart I sourced from the Internet but is a count I have seen on many 
occasions, albeit with some slight differences to the wave count.) Alternation 
between Wave 1 and Wave 4 rarely generate any shallow/deep or deep/shallow 
pattern. The Wave B (in which the (a)(b)(c) was a running correction within 
which the Wave c (in the center of the chart labeled (b)) was probably around 
33.3%-38.2% of Wave a. Personally I find this rather strange. At the end of the 
chart (which was posted before the low I have labeled Wave iv in my chart) it 
suggests that the final high was in place and that there should be a return to the 
2009 low. 
There is another important difference between the two charts in Fig 5-l .  As 
mentioned above, if you can imagine how the analyst decided where the waves 
were going to complete, well, they had to be in retrospect. The Wave 5 's are of 
different lengths, some similar to those I use in Harmonic Elliott Wave and some 


=== PAGE 96 ===

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 91 
that are extended. In the case of the Wave (b) following the Wave a high and 
correction in Wave b, how could it have been possible to anticipate such a short 
Wave c? How would it be known that the Wave ( 5) would develop as an ending 
triangle? How would it be known that Wave 1 would extend? In short, I do not 
see how it would be possible to even have an idea of where the wave will 
complete. 
The Harmonic Elliott Wave chart in Fig 5-1 highlights not only the consistency 
of alternation but also of a consistent structure. In addition, the fact that I have 
forecast the major turning points (from mid 201 0) in the rally highlights the 
efficacy of the Harmonic Elliott Wave structure.

Fig 5-2. A rally in EURJPY through three wave degrees 
Fig 5-2 displays a complicated rally in EURJPY. While ignoring the Wave (i)

is quite straightforward in Harmonic Elliott Wave. At first sight it would be 
necessary to scan the structure to note the deep versus shallow corrections and 
where they match. Throughout the rally the alternation was perfect. 
The process can be quite frustrating at times, with a great deal of noise in the 
lower degrees that makes following the count live quite challenging. When this 
begins to develop and cause some doubts, it is always useful to backtrack every 
now and then to check the count through the lower degree waves.

Trending moves will normally hold strong alternation

Fig 5-3 displays a sharp rally in the daily chart (inset at the top left of the chart) 
which is broken down in the hourly chart. Normally, when I see such a direct 
move, I tend to first consider the potential for a 3-wave move. The reason for this 
initial assumption is because the lack of deep corrections - the areas that would 
satisfy alternation tend to imply there are two 5-wave moves, each with their 
Wave (ii) I (iv) relationships. The daily chart above doesn't really offer that 
option because from a Wave [iv] we need to see two 5-wave rallies in Wave [a] 
and Wave [c] to complete Wave [v]. 
Fig 5-3. The breakdown of a 5-wave rally in daily GBPUSD 
The daily chart may be difficult to see but is basically a long and very direct 
rally in a Wave [a] of Wave [ v] position. There is virtually no significant 
correction as the rally continues. As can be seen, there is only one deeper 
corrective wave and that is right at the start of the rally, being 74%. While there 
are a few Wave ii corrections that are deep, in terms of relevance to the entire 
rally, they are less significant. As you begin to work with the Harmonic Elliott 
Wave impulsive structure you will begin to notice the general balance within the 
structure. 
In Fig 5-3, I mentioned that I normally make an assumption that a direct move 
with limited corrections is more likely to be a 3-wave structure. However, Fig 5-4 
is just an example of an occasion in the sharp decline in EURUSD from the 2008 
1 .6036 high.

Fig 5-5. A 3-wave decline in EURUSD from the 1 .6036 high 
Fig 5-5 displays the decline in Fig 5-4 from 1 .6036 in the 4-hour chart. This 
may be difficult to see and I would have preferred to use an hourly chart, but 
wanted to provide the entire 3-wave decline within one chart. I have provided the 


=== PAGE 99 ===

94 I GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
labeling and ratios for the primary count but have not done so for the lower 
degree counts mainly because it would have looked too busy with the number of 
ratios. However, each 5-wave move in a Wave -a-, Wave a, Wave -c- and Wave c 
were all measured to confirm the internal fractals maintained the normal 
projection and retracement ratios, also ensuring alternation was present between 
each Wave ii and Wave iv retracements respectively. 
The point I am trying to convey here is that: simply seeing a sharp move and 
making an assumption that it is a single impulsive wave, will be detrimental to 
understanding what is happening. Such an assumption can lead to false counts. 
Clearly, with no ratios there will be no ability to know where targets will be. In 
other words, it is a total lottery and in the end retrospective. 
IMPORTANT: 
Always check the internal count to confirm 
I am therefore always delighted when traders/analysts like the approach I have 
in Harmonic Elliott Wave and see them attempting to apply the method. 
However, if I see any consistent mistake, it is to organize a wave count that 
appears correct - but within which the internal Wave a and Wave c are not 
checked for valid 5-wave sequences with appropriate ratios. Indeed, it is easy to 
trick yourself because the count placed on the chart appears to have valid ratios. 
Do remember that these ratios can conveniently tum up but the internal count is 
not valid. 
Fig 5-6. An apparent 5-wave rally in the S&P 500 Index with valid ratios 


=== PAGE 100 ===

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 1 95 
I have received queries from traders trying to interpret the S&P 500 Index as 
shown in Fig 5-6. Just looking at the swing highs and swing lows, and labeling 
them, produces a structure that looks really good, all ratios relevant for each wave 
and with alternation intact. However, the problem has been the failure to work 
through the lower degree waves and confirm the internal ratios. 
Just considering the Wave (i) marked, already in Fig 3 - 1 1 I have confirmed 
that it was a 5-wave rally in a Wave (a) rather than 3-wave. Also, the rally from 
the Wave (iv) in Fig 5-6 up to the Wave (a), Wave (b) and eventual Wave (c) I 
(v), was described in Fig 3-13 that provided a complete 5-wave rally. Equally, in 
the rally from Wave (iv) to Wave (a) there is no valid alternation. 
This does highlight the combination of the correct ratios throughout all Wave 
(a) and Wave (c) moves must be measured and must have valid alternation. This 
is required from the 5-minute charts through monthly charts that all comply with 
the same ratio structure and alternation. 
Fig 5-7 A rally in AUDUSD but displaying a 5-wave Wave v 
Another example of errors that can be made is shown in Fig 5-7 in which a 
rally is developing in Wave [a] of Wave [iii]. However, clearly circled to the top 
right, the Wave v has developed in 5-waves and not in 3-waves. To confirm that, 
the final leg higher also developed in 3-waves that would imply it was a lower 
degree Wave v. Without observing closely the lower degree development the 
risks of misjudgments rises considerably. 
This may deter analysts from using Harmonic Elliott Wave, many claiming 
that such accuracy is not required and why do they need to try and find accurate 
targets. The answer is quite simple. Firstly, by ensuring fractal development and 


=== PAGE 101 ===

96 I GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
the resultant Wave (iii) targets in particular, it is possible to be more confident of 
the anticipated wave structure under development, avoids a great deal of 
uncertainty and enables a stronger level of money management. 
In the run up to the final high in the U.S. equity markets I have observed so 
many analysts and traders claiming a high must soon be in place - this from back 
in 20 14. Even towards the end of the rally analysts were still not sure whether 
there would be a new high above the 1 8,35 1 .36 high (which was actually the 
Wave (a) of Wave (v) ) . From the Harmonic Elliott Wave structure it was clearly 
evident that a new high was required . . .

I have seen on many occasions, a particularly sharp move, quite often 
following a key economic release that triggers a frenetic reaction by the market. 
When I've seen traditional Elliotticians approach this development they tend to 
look for (in a rally as shown in Fig 5-8) the initial highest high, which in this 
example is at Wave i and label it Wave (1). 
Harmonic Elliot Wave can provide logical expectations 
However, this is where Harmonic Elliott Wave excels in truly catching the 
structure. Following the Wave (ii) a Wave i develops, followed by a deep Wave ii 


=== PAGE 102 ===

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 1 97 
and from that we will know that Wave iv will be shallow. This is followed by the 
Wave v to form Wave (a) and then yet another shallow correction in Wave (b). 
The Wave i of Wave (c) makes another new high, and in this example, a shallow 
Wave ii which we can then automatically know will provoke a deeper Wave iv, 
potentially a complex. 
In this manner, we have expectations provided for us and with the use of the 
Harmonic Elliott Wave spreadsheet can anticipate potential projections.

Fig 5-9. A sharp decline in the daily GBPUSD chart 
As an example, Fig 5-9 displays a 5-wave decline in daily GBPUSD. In Wave 
(c) of Wave (iii) the first Wave ii developed as an expanded flat with Wave efc 
retracing very deeply. As the wave development continued, the resultant Wave iv 
was shallow and formed a simple zigzag. This is an extremely useful technique to 
use and generates a great deal of confidence in expectations. Not only does this 
provide expectations but will also highlight, very quickly, if something goes 
wrong at the Wave iii and Wave (iii) projection targets. 
When looking at a daily chart only, with the lower degree development hidden 
within the daily bars, it is important to observe the individual bars together with 
the bar open and close ticks. Every now and then, a bar that declined strongly 
may have an open tick and close tick at the top of the bar. That is a clue that there 
had been a significant intra-day whiplash. It may imply a Wave i and Wave ii but 
that could generate a longer Wave iv later.

Fig 5-9a. The decline in Fig 5-9 broken down into the 1 20-minute chart 
While quite often the daily chart can hide the internal structure of the lower 
degrees, I have taken this decline and used a lower granularity in order to be able 
to see the decline in one chart. The development of the structure can be seen with 
the relevant ratios. In particular, within Wave (c) of Wave (iii), note the expanded 
flat Wave ii of 83% that was followed by a minor Wave iv - hardly noticeable in 
the 1 20-minute chart - but with perfect alternation (deep complex versus shallow 
simple) before losses in Wave v to reach the Wave (iii) projection target. 
This process can be quite difficult to follow in a 5-minute chart that can 
contain a great deal of lower degree noise, but at all times we need understand 
what is required for each stage of the structural development and how the wave 
degrees combine harmoniously with each other. 
LOWER DEGREE NOISE 
There are two situations that can cause significant problems in terms of looking 
for the foundation Waves i and ii. The first is a plethora of moves within a tight 
range and the second is the exact opposite, a complete lack of noise due to an 
extreme sharp movement or a series of mini -corrections which makes being 
confident of what these waves represent, difficult to decipher. Given that these 
waves are vital to identify, in order to be able to estimate the Wave iii and Wave 
iv in the spreadsheet, this is a very crucial matter. There are a few techniques that 
can be employed, some as they are developing and others after more development 
has been seen. 
I'll try and provide a few examples.

Noise in the lower timeframe charts can be frustrating and at times can even 
leave me still with doubts. If this does occur it's best to either, wait and allow the 
price development to continue or if this is mid-wave then you at least have an 
idea of the expected direction. 
In Fig 5-10 the same development in AUDUSD can be seen in two timeframes. 
On the left is the 5-minute chart in which I customarily use as a base to build the 
higher timeframe analysis. After determining the foundation Wave (i) and Wave 
(ii) (and even that does not show a great depth of information in the 5-minute 
chart), it can be seen that the key waves are not necessarily forming swing highs 
and swing lows. These tend to be ignored by traditional Elliotticians, who 
normally want to see swing highs and swing lows irrespective of timeframes. In 
many ways, it is possible to "get away" with ignoring it, but when it comes to the 
daily charts it will make a difference to the outcome. The chart to the right is the 
1-minute development. In this, the Wave -ii- is very clearly a swing low. Equally, 
the first Wave iv (light gray) could be ignored because it looks like a continuation 
of the larger move. However, in the 1 -minute chart there is, even if small, a 
correction with a swing low. 
It is noticeable that the ratio clusters for each wave adhere to the general 
findings I have outlined when discussing the ratio structures in Chapter 3 -
including alternation. Having said that, it is the extreme lower degree 
development that can prove to be a lot more difficult when there is a sudden, 
sharp movement, as is often seen following key economic releases. 
On a final point on the development in Fig 5-10, the development in Wave -c­
of Wave -iii- is not particularly clear. However, by going down to the 1 -minute 
chart, the structure becomes apparent and where more sense can be made of the 
apparent noise. This technique can be used across all timeframe charts. Too often 


=== PAGE 105 ===

100 I GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
I am sent wave counts in the daily charts that are incorrect because the daily bars 
hide the lower degree development. 
ESTABLISHING A STRUCTURE FOLLOWING A VAGUE WAVE (i) 
One big tip in deciphering challenging price development is: What wave is due 
next? 5-wave or 3-wave? 
If you can recognize a subsequent 5-wave move it will be possible to look 
more closely at the initial development from the start in order to see whether there 
was a 3-wave move to start with. 
Fig 5-1 1 . Uncertainty at the beginning of a 5-wave decline 
There are quite a few occasions where there is uncertainty over whether the 
first move is Wave (a) of Wave (i) or the completed Wave (i). Fig 5-1 1 displays a 
sharp decline, almost entirely in one 5-minute bar from the Wave [ii] high. There 
is a correction and then second decline before a retracement back to just above 
the first corrective high. Here, we have two decisions to make, or at least 
consider. Was that first sharp decline the Wave (i) or was it just the entire Wave 
(a) followed by a correction and then the drift lower to form Wave (c) I (i)? 
The first option (that we had seen Wave (i)) would imply an expanded flat in 
Wave (ii), while the second option would be an clear ABC move with the Wave 
(c) being 63% (which is a bit short for a normal Wave (c), although there have 


=== PAGE 106 ===

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 101 
been occasions where I have seen them.) For clarity we'd have to look at the 
structure in the lower timeframe charts. 
At this point I did look at a lower degree chart, although on this occasion I 
chose a 1 0-tick bar chart in Fig 5-l la. This implies each bar is constructed of 10 
updates (ticks) instead of using a time-based method. Well, there still wasn't 
much information. However, from what I could see, this was not an impulsive 
decline and therefore I judged it to be a series of corrective waves in Wave EFB 
of an expanded flat Wave (ii).

Fig 5-1 1a. The 10-tick bar chart of the initial decline in Fig 5-1 1 
Having made that decision, but perhaps still having some doubts, confirming 
the initial low being a Wave (i) will require us to ensure that we see a 5-wave 
decline - from the Wave (ii) - to form Wave (a) of Wave (iii). From Fig 5-1 1 ,  the 
high from which we are analyzing was a Wave [ii] of a larger Wave (C) I (III) 
and was therefore part of an impulsive decline. Consequently, the decline under 
development from the expanded flat Wave (ii) must be a Wave (a) and therefore 
should develop in 5-waves. The point here is that we need to confirm that we then 
subsequently see 5-waves lower. 
As can be seen in Fig 5-1 1 ,  this 5-wave decline developed really well. Indeed, 
this impulsive move was part of the decline, coming from the 1 .  7 1 90 high in July 
2014 and that ended a weekly double zigzag from the 1 .3501 low. This 
automatically provides us with a target - the span of the prior daily Wave (b). 
Also, because the 1 .7190 high was a major monthly Wave (B), this will require a 
5-wave decline in Wave (C) - and below the Wave (A) at 1 .3501 - and as will be 
seen in Chapter Eight, could reach as low as the 1 .0370 low that was seen in 


=== PAGE 107 ===

102 I GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
January 1 985. Consequently, we know that we must first identify a target for the 
major Wave (I) that will form the first wave in the larger Wave (C) and which 
could later allow a Wave (III), (IV) and (V) to reach the expected target. 
With this information we can begin to understand the rough potential for this 
decline that needs to form Wave (A) of the Wave (I) (and that the Wave (I) will 
probably reach - approximately - the prior daily Wave (b) - seen in the inset of 
Fig 5-1 1 b). The process of understanding where the approximate target for a 
move must reach is a very important one. It can guide the lower degree analysis 
when there is some doubt over any uncertain initial wave, such as an excessively 
sharp move. Hence, when in this situation, it is always best to observe the higher 
degree structure to judge the requirements for the lower degree fractals. 
Having identified the Wave (a), the next task is to confirm a second 5-wave 
decline in Wave (c) that must be a valid projection in Wave (iii).

Once again, we are left with an even more difficult structure that, following the 
Wave (b), the next decline was once again, exceptionally sharp with multiple 
shallow corrections. At this stage, because we are expecting losses and also 
ensuring that momentum indicators are still pointing lower (which I shall cover in 
a later chapter,) we will have to run with this decline and attempt to identify 5-
wave moves again to establish the structure and (hopefully) be able to identify the 
correct structure. It can be seen that deep corrections are not in the first two Wave 
ii positions (black and grey - just below the Wave (b)), and at some point we 
must therefore expect a deeper correction to satisfy alternation. However, 
following the lower degree Wave ii (following the 3 1% correction in Wave b) we 


=== PAGE 108 ===

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES I 103 
know that it will imply a shallow Wave iv. It will probably only be at that point 
where we can be more certain of the requirement for the earlier lower degree 
waves. Therefore, from the lowest Wave iii low in the bottom-center of the chart 
that we would have to go back to work out the lower degree waves. 
In this example the ratios were solid except the Wave iv retraced only 50% to 
generate a sum of Wave ii and Wave iv of only 68%. Indeed, it couldn't get much 
higher otherwise it would reach too close to the Wave b of Wave iii. There are 
occasions when alternation fails. It's not very frequent but in these circumstances 
we need to ensure the lower degree waves are valid and with appropriate ratios. 
Eventually, the Wave v extended lower by just over 4 1 .4% to reach 2 points 
away from the 638.2% projection in Wave (iii).

Fig 5-l lc. The resultant Wave (v) in the decline in GBPUSD 
Following the Wave (iii) there was a long sideways Wave (iv) of only 14.6% 
that saw a sum of Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) of 76%. Again, this is disappointing 
but minor shortfalls occasionally happen. The point of describing this example is 
more to understand how to recognize two factors: 
-
Whether to expect a 5-wave (or 3-wave) development so that you can 
place them in the current wave to slot into the larger degree wave. 
-
Having an understanding of the approximate strength/target of the Wave 
iii in order that it is appropriate for the larger wave degree target. 
By doing this it can help in ensuring - even through retrospectively identifying 
5-wave moves - that the impulsive development is appropriate for the larger 
degree structure. 


=== PAGE 109 ===

104 I GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
ALTERNATION FAILURE 
In the above example in Fig 5-1 1 c  we did have a failed alternation, the sum of 
Wave ii and Wave iv being just 68%. As mentioned previously, this can occur 
from time to time although I do prefer to see either a deep Wave b of Wave iii or 
deep Wave b of Wave v to compensate for the lack of alternation. However, in 
the example above neither happened but this is not a frequent event. 
What I would like to cover next are the occasions when I have forecast a Wave 
(iii) - and therefore also the Wave (iv) by using the general relationship between 
Wave (ii) and Wave (iv) - but the Wave (iv) falls short in the depth of the 
correction. In the majority of cases, this is caused by the fact that the Wave (iii) 
target was hit precisely but was actually the Wave iii of Wave (c) I (iii). This has 
happened to me on several occasions, having me cheer as my target was 
coincidently hit precisely, but was then followed by a shallower correction than 
expected and a resumption of the impulsive move to the next higher projection 
target. From this stronger Wave (iii) target the correction did satisfy alternation.

When I have encountered this type of event, it is normally in the 5-minute chart

only seen Wave a of Wave v and the Wave c hit a deeper Wave v target that 
moved to the next impulsive projection. This type of event doesn't occur often 
but it's worth keeping this in mind as a solution. 
WORKING WITH ALTERNATION 
As I mentioned earlier, alternation is a fantastic tool. It is one element that 
really sets aside Harmonic Elliott Wave against the traditional Elliott Wave. 
Indeed, from most Elliotticians I have observed, it's not even in their toolbox. 
However, it does require attention to detail. In Fig 5-13 I have displayed the same 
development in both charts, the chart on the left being the 5-minute chart, which I 
habitually use as my base timeframe for analysis, and on the right, the 1 -minute 
chart. 


=== PAGE 111 ===

106 I GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
In the left chart, from just above the second Wave (b), there is a rally that stalls 
at a small consolidation area which has been circled. Observe carefully that the 
high labeled Wave a and subsequent low, labeled Wave b. Following the Wave b, 
price shoots higher and corrects deeply. This is a clue. Note the sharp manner in 
which the subsequent rally develops.

Fig 5-13. Noting alternation can guide towards an impulsive structure 
In the left chart, from just above the second Wave (b), there is a rally that stalls 
at a small consolidation area which has been circled. Observe carefully that the 
high labeled Wave a and subsequent low, labeled Wave b. Following the Wave b, 
price shoots higher and corrects deeply. This is a clue. Note the sharp manner in 
which the subsequent rally develops. 
Again, this type of development is normally overlooked by Elliotticians who 
habitually look for key swing highs and lows. This development is highlighted in 
the chart to the right of Fig 5-13. Just to point out, I have provided the ratios to 
demonstrate how accurate they were, the 1 00% retracement in Wave ii matching 
a 35% retracement in Wave iv. This (gray) sequence slotted into the higher 
degree wave showing valid projection ratios within the fractal sequence. 
I have been accused of "analysis paralysis" but perhaps I can counter by 
pointing out that, while this was in the 5 -minute and 1-minute charts, the same 
development is possible in all higher degree charts. In this example, the structure 
was highlighted clearly and which had reached a Wave iii target to the point. 
Once the anticipated Wave iv support had broken the bullish structure, the fact 
that it we had seen an ABC in Wave i and a second ABC, implied that instead of 
an impulsive rally it had turned into a double zigzag that would slot into a 
corrective structure. 


=== PAGE 112 ===

GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES i 107 
In other words, it brings clarity and understanding so that the analyst can judge 
the alternatives. Without that understanding the potential for mistakes rises and 
the understanding of where one alternative breaks down to retain the other 
alternative - but still with an understanding where it breaks down. Knowledge of 
these break areas is vital. 
ANTICIPATING A WAVE (iv) FOLLOWING A SHALLOW WAVE (ii) 
This is a relatively easy technique that permits identifying a Wave (iii) 
projection target area. This is a direct benefit from the concept of alternation. 
Very clearly, if Wave (ii) is around 23.6%-33.3% the Wave (iv) will need to be 
between 50% and 58.6%. By utilizing the spreadsheet, it is simple to work out the 
minimum Wave (iii) projection ratio that would identify the required 50%-58.6% 
retracement in Wave (iv) to ensure that it remains above the Wave (b) of Wave 
(iii). 
Let's take the example of the rally from the 2009 low in the Dow Jones 
Industrial Index, seen in Fig 5-14, being the forecast I made in my book, 
Harmonic Elliott Wave.

In Fig 5-15a the next higher section of the spreadsheet is shown with the 
starting point from 9,614.32 being the Wave (b) of Wave (iii) seen in Fig 5-15. 
As this progressed, the Wave -iii- reached just short of the 223.6% projection, 
corrected by just over 33.3% in Wave -iv- and from there we could look at the 
Wave -v- targets that would match with the Wave (iii) targets. The 50% 
projection was at 12,943.97 which is a little too far above the 198.7% but below 
the 223.6% which was at 13,47 1 .58. This would require Wave -v- to move above 
the 66.7% projection. The next higher Wave (iii) projection at 238.2% was at 
13,823.15 but this would have required a Wave -v- above 76.4% and was 
approaching the 14, 19 8.1 0 high. 
Therefore, the two most likely options were the 198.7% projection in Wave 
(iii) or the 223.6%. Thus, once Wave -iv- was established there was a need to 
observe the lower degree development in Wave -a-, the correction in Wave -b­
and followed by the Wave -c- to determine which of the two targets would finally 
occur in Wave -v-. 
Therefore, when a shallow Wave (ii) develops, do make sure that the 
anticipated Wave (iv) will remain above the Wave (b) of Wave (iii) by testing 
various Wave (iii) projection ratios in the Harmonic Elliott Wave spreadsheet that 
would see the deep Wave (iv) stall in a valid area. Indeed, while this is a 
particularly critical issue with any Wave (ii), it is always necessary to ensure that 
the Wave (iv), even if less than 50%, must remain above/below the Wave (b) of 
Wave (iii) by a safe margin. 
WHEN A WAVE COUNT LOOKS RIGHT BUT FAILS 
There are times when following a count that develops perfectly - until the 
unexpected happens and a Wave -iv- doesn't work. It obviously depends on 
where this wave fits into the higher degree structure because through Harmonic 
Elliott Wave we will have expectations for targets. Here I shall demonstrate how 
a count looks perfect but fails. This occurred in a decline in GBPUSD. 
The problem developed in the Wave [ c] of Wave [ v] following the decline 
from the 1 .7 190 high in July 201 4  seen in Fig 5-16. I observed the decline from 
the Wave [b] high and considered the initial losses developed in 5-waves. I 
therefore labeled this Wave -a- and followed by firmer losses in Wave -c- to form 
Wave -i-. The pullback in Wave -ii- was around 47.2% and followed by a 5-wave 
decline in Wave -a- and Wave -b- before another 5-wave decline to complete 
Wave -iii-. With the Wave -c- being 1 05% and the Wave -iii- 278.6%, the

The cause of the incorrect wave count in Fig 5-16 is quite a common one but 
quite often the solution does not require a significant adjustment. The decline 
from the Wave [b] was quite brief. When I had observed this bearish wave in the 
5-minute chart I felt it looked more like a 5-wave decline and therefore labeled it 
Wave -a-, followed by a Wave -b- and then down to Wave -c- I -i-. 
In the solution following the break above, what I thought should have been a 
deep Wave -iv-, was to shift the Wave -i- to where the Wave -a- had been. The 
rest of the decline was basically left in place, being 5-wave declines, with a small 
adjustment to where I had placed Wave -a- and Wave -b- of Wave -iii-. The shift 
to the right for the Wave -iii- and Wave -iv- in Fig 5-1 8  made more sense because 
the prior correction acted as the Wave iv of Wave v to reach the Wave -c- I -iii-. 
It is not uncommon in these initial waves, as a new direction begins, that can 
cause some problems. Often, we can estimate the depth of the Wave -i- required,

that after the Wave -ii-, to generate a Wave -iii- target that is appropriate to reach

whether a full 5-wave decline will stall at the prior Wave (b) of Wave (v) or just 
the lower degree Wave -iii- that will see the Wave -iv- and Wave -v- extend the 
first wave beyond the prior Wave (b) of Wave (v). Even when going back and 
looking at the 5-minute development of that initial wave I find that development 
difficult to judge. In this case it would require viewing the 1 -minute charts.

Indeed, the corrective count is far more likely because the initial decline from

The initial decline from 1 .6036 came in 3-waves and was followed by a large

appear in a Wave (II) position. 
We can employ cycles to judge whether a new low is likely. On this point, I 
cannot use EURUSD for cycles because the history is far too short and any 
synthetic history is invalid because the method of quoting EURUSD is indirect 
compared to pre-EUR when the individual European currencies were quoted 
directly. For this purpose I will use GBPUSD.

UNDERSTANDING WHEN ANALYSIS HAS VULNERABILITIES 
This array of complementary analysis provides us with a much stronger 
framework from which a more informed expectation can be drawn. Referring 
back to Fig 5-2 1  above, when there was a clear bullish divergence but which 
failed, very clearly the count was wrong. Having got to this point, what can be 
done to correct this? 
First of all, there needs to be a thorough review of the count. Consideration 
must be given to where alternation may lie. Within the lower degree charts there 
were several 5-wave moves that developed. The question to ask is then, "How did 
they fit in with the structure around these 5-wave moves?" Clearly a 5-wave 
move must be a Wave -a- or Wave -c-, and therefore the corrections before and 
after these waves can be observed to see if alternation is present. This is a very 
useful tool in determining the likely count by "working backwards" from a 
development within which a clearer structure is constructed and relevant . to 
surrounding waves. Utilizing the spreadsheet is an excellent tool to confirm a 
valid structure. 


=== PAGE 123 ===

118 I GENERAL TIPS ON WORKING WITH WAVES 
Fig 5-25. When divergences appear but fail to be confirmed. 
On two occasions, I had come to a point where I thought a reversal was 
expected. These are very awkward times, normally approached with an apparent 
valid structure, but then begins to develop in a way where the divergence persists, 
thus making it difficult to consider looking for an alternative structure. In the case 
of Fig 5-24, to tum bearish, while both hourly and 4-hour momentum were still 
displaying bullish divergences appears conflicting. 
This does highlight two points: 
-
That divergences do need to be confirmed. 
-
That there will be times where the analysis becomes bogged down with 
conflicting indications. 
Normally, this is a sign that the wave count is incorrect and needs a thorough 
overhaul. As mentioned above, this can be managed by referring to the 5-wave 
moves, but sometimes it needs a tweak here or there, normally involving the 
identification of potential alternation between lower degree Wave ii and Wave 
iv's. Having made those changes it is vital that the following development 
proceeds as expected. 
Overall, even though I had to make changes, had a couple of areas that saw 
price drag lower when I was expecting a correction higher, the overall 
expectation was still bearish but I had to bring the sequences back into valid 
impulsive ratios throughout. Once I had realized that the decline had reached a 
low that would mean that we were not seeing a clean Wave (a), Wave (b) and 
Wave (c) to form Wave (i) the process became more stable.

Chapter Six
